Central Limit Theorem for Sequential Dynamical Systems Thanks: This work was supported by the Grant (PRIN 2017S35EHN) and by the MIUR Excellence Department Project MatMod@TOV. C.L. acknowledges membership to the GNFM/INDAM and support from the Brin Center, University of Maryland. M.D. was partially supported by National Science Foundation Grants DMS 2055070 and DMS 2350079. Finally, we would like to thank Dmitry Dolgopyat for many valuable discussions.
Mark F. Demers, Carlangelo Liverani

Abstract
. We present a general approach to establish the Central Limit Theorem with error bounds for sequential dynamical systems. The main tool we develop is the application to this setting of a projective metric on complex cones, following the ideas introduced by Rugh and Dubois. To demonstrate the power of the proposed setting, we apply it to both sequential expanding maps, where similar results are known, and to sequential dispersing billiards, for which no such results are currently known.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 37A50, 60F05; Secondary 37D20, 37D40

1.
Introduction

This paper is concerned with obtaining a self-normed Central Limit Theorem with error terms for
sequential dynamical systems.
Such results are relevant for many open problems; let us just mention random
Lorentz gases of the type initially presented in
[
25
]
, in which a particle moves between cells formed by randomly placed obstacles
in an approximately
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
lattice. In each cell, the particle finds a different arrangement of scatterers.
From the particle’s point of
view, it undergoes a sequence of different billiard maps corresponding to the cells it visits. Such a model was
considered in several works
[
25
,
26
,
1
]
and most recently in
[
8
]
, which introduced the application
of Birkhoff cones to dispersing billiards.
More broadly, many results regarding the Central Limit Theorem for sequential systems already exist, for example,
[
4
,
34
,
33
,
20
,
21
,
27
,
22
,
32
,
38
,
15
]
in which are investigated either random dynamical systems or sequential expanding maps, possibly non-uniformly expanding.

In the present paper, we present a general setting to establish a self-normed Central Limit Theorem with error terms for sequential dynamical systems, which allows us to treat both observables that are not bounded and discontinuous hyperbolic dynamical systems, including dispersing billiards. The results are obtained via the study of the properties of the corresponding transfer operators. As we are interested in presenting the general strategy, we do not push for optimal results. Yet, since some estimates rest on a Taylor expansion, better results can be obtained by computing more terms. Note, however, that to obtain optimal results, it is necessary to have an estimate of the
higher momenta in terms of the second momenta, and it is not clear how to achieve this in the present generality (see Remark
2.10
for a more detailed explanation
of this issue).

In Section
2
, we present two sets of abstract hypotheses: the first hypothesis assumes only that the real transfer operator is contracting in a projective metric, together with a uniform bound on the complex twisted transfer operator; the second hypothesis assumes that the complex transfer operator is also contracting. The second possibility yields much sharper results. We explain in Section
5
under which conditions the latter extra information on the transfer operator can be obtained via the theory of complex cones introduced by Rugh
[
35
]
and further developed by Dubois
[
16
,
17
]
. Since this theory is of independent interest and is spread among several articles, for the reader’s convenience, we provide a short, self-contained account of the theory in this section. Our presentation is not as
general as the one that can be found in
[
35
,
16
,
17
]
, but it is more than sufficient for all the applications
to the Central Limit Theorem we can think of.

To illustrate the broad applicability of our result, we apply it to some examples; indeed more than half of the paper is devoted to the examples. To avoid a lengthy and repetitive list of possible applications, we consider two emblematic cases: smooth expanding maps (the simplest possibility) and hyperbolic billiards (the most technical one). We are sure the reader can fill in other relevant applications as needed.
The application to expanding maps in Section
6
is done to compare our present
Theorems
2.6
and
2.7
with existing results. We show that our technique reproduces all existing
results apart from the ones in
[
14
]
, which just appeared. In addition, in Section
6.5
we provide a new criterion to verify the growth of the variance. This is an issue not usually addressed in the literature but fundamental in applications. The strategy used in
[
14
]
is similar to ours when applied to the case in which one can use complex cones but takes advantage of the fact that it deals only with bounded observables and expanding maps. In particular, it uses a martingale decomposition trick, adapted from
[
31
, Chapter 3]
, in order to estimate higher momenta via the second momenta. It is unclear how to extend such a trick to the level of generality we are addressing in the present work. Possibly, something can be done using ideas in
[
29
, Theorem 1.2]
applied to the stable foliation of the sequential maps, but as the present paper is already rather long, we refrain from exploring this issue.
The application to billiards in Section
7
is brand new and shows the generality and power of the theory put forward in this paper. We expect this application to be relevant for the study of random Lorentz gasses, a long-standing open problem.

2.
Setting and Main Results

Before stating the abstract framework in which we are able to study the Central Limit Theorem for sequential systems, we briefly describe two applications:
smooth expanding maps and dispersing billiards. This gives the reader an indication of the broad applicability of the present
approach.

2.1.
Sequential Smooth Expanding Maps: A Preview

The case of expanding maps has been extensively studied, and our general theory does not provide any new results, apart from the discussion of the growth of the variance. We discuss this case in Section
6
only so the reader can compare the present results with the literature. However, even in this case, we are able to present new results for the problem of studying the variance.

Let
M
be a smooth compact connected Riemannian manifold.
Let
(f_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset C^{2}(M,M)
be a uniform family of expanding maps in the following sense:
\exists A>0
,
\vartheta>1
such that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

•

\|Df_{k}^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq\vartheta^{-1}
;

•

\|D^{2}f_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq A
.

Let
g_{k}\in C^{1}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
with
\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|g_{k}\|_{C^{1}}\leq C
for some
C>0
.
Given an initial density
\rho\in L^{1}
, we have that
g_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdot\circ f_{0}
is a random variable. We designate its expectation by

{\mathbb{E}}(g_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0})=\int_{M}g_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(x)\rho(x)dx,

and call
{\mathbb{P}}
the associated probability distribution.
Define the centered observables,

(2.1)

\begin{split}\hat{g}_{k}&=g_{k}-\int_{M}g_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\cdot\rho\\
\hat{S}_{n}&=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\,\,;\qquad\sigma_{n}^{2}=\mathbb{E}(\hat{S}_{n}^{2})\,,\end{split}

and the cumulative distribution function

(2.2)

F_{n}(x)=\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\frac{\hat{S}_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\leq x\right\}\right).

The above, apart from some minor quirks, is the same model discussed in
[
4
,
34
,
21
]
.
In Section
6.1
we will prove the following.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(M)
. Then for all
c_{\star}\in(0,1)
and all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sigma_{n}\geq\max\{1,c_{\star}n^{\frac{1}{3}}\ln(n+1)\}
, there exists
\bar{C}>0
such that

\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}dy\right|\leq\bar{C}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}n.

The above Theorem can be applied only if
\sigma_{n}
grows fast enough. As mentioned, in the special case of expanding maps, it is possible to strengthen the result, see
[
14
]
. However, it is always necessary to meet some requirements on the growth of
\sigma_{n}
.

Unfortunately, contrary to the case of a single map, we are not aware of an existing result that provides general checkable criteria to ensure some variance growth. By checkable, we mean that one can ensure the wanted variance growth by computing only averages on a finite number of finite time trajectories.

In Proposition
6.1
, we provide such a criterion for the case of smooth expanding maps. Our criterion naturally generalizes the usual criterion for a single map. While the argument is rather general, some technical problems do not allow, at the moment, to extend it to the general setting in which we obtain the CLT.

2.2.
Sequential Dispersing Billiards: A Preview

We consider a class of dispersing billiard tables formed by a fixed number
\ell
of closed, convex sets, which we call scatterers, on the
torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. The boundaries of the scatterers are assumed to have strictly positive curvature.
The dynamics of the billiard map are defined by the motion of a point particle traveling at unit speed between collisions
and reflecting elastically at collisions with the scatterers.
We consider
the family of billiard maps
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
corresponding to configurations of
\ell
scatterers which satisfy

•

The minimum and maximum free flight times between collisions are bounded by
\tau_{*}
and
\tau_{*}^{-1}
, respectively.

•

The minimum and maximum curvature of the boundary of the scatterers are bounded by
{\mathcal{K}}_{*}
and

{\mathcal{K}}_{*}^{-1}
, respectively.

•

The maximum of the
C^{3}
norm of the scatterers is bounded by
E_{*}
.

All billiard maps in
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
act on the same phase space
M
and preserve the same invariant
measure
d\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}=c_{0}\cos\varphi\,drd\varphi
, where
c_{0}
is the normalizing constant.

Given this family
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
, we prove a sequential Central Limit Theorem
along any
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
-admissible sequence
of maps
(T_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
.
See Definition
7.1
for the definition of
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
-admissible sequence.
1
1

1

Informally,
given
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in\mathbb{N}
,
an
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
-admissible sequence is a sequence of billiard maps drawn from the family
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})

which comprise blocks of length
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
in which all maps in each block are close to one another, but maps in different blocks are not necessarily close.

For the Central Limit Theorem, we consider sequences of observables
g_{k}\in C^{\alpha}(M)
for some
\alpha\in(0,1/3)

with
|g_{k}|_{C^{\alpha}}\leq K
for some
K>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Given a strictly positive density
\rho\in C^{1}
,
2
2

2

The results holds more generally for
\rho\in C_{{\mathbb{R}}}
; see
Section
7.1
for the precise definition of
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}
.

d\nu_{0}=\rho d\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}
, and denote
by
\mathbb{E}
the expectation with respect to
\nu_{0}
. We define the centered variables exactly as in (
2.1
) (where the integrals are w.r.t.
\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}
) and the cumulative distribution function as in (
2.2
). Our results can be summarized as follows (see Theorem
7.12
for a precise statement, while the beginning of Section
7
contains a precise definition of the sequential system and Section
7.4
a detailed description of the observables).

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
be as defined above and let
(T_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})

be an
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
-admissible sequence, and
\ln\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
,
\int\rho\,d\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}=1
.

Then for all
c_{\star}\in(0,1)
there exists
\bar{C}>0
such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sigma_{n}\geq\max\{1,c_{\star}n^{1/3}\ln(n+1)\}
,

\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}\,dy\right|\leq\bar{C}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}n.

Note that in the non-sequential case, when
T_{k}=T
and
g_{k}=g
, if
g
is not a coboundary, then
\sigma_{n}^{2}\sim n
. Hence the error term is
{\mathcal{O}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2})
which is optimal, apart from the logarithm.

In the sequential case we do not have general criteria to check that
\sigma_{n}\geq\max\{1,c_{\star}n^{1/3}\ln(n+1)\}
(apart from the case in which the billiards are extremely hyperbolic, that is the operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
have a large spectral gap
3
3

3

We thank Dmitry Dolgopyat for pointing this out to us.
).
However, sharper results can be obtained in the random case, see Section
7.9
where we improve on the usual condition (cf. Remark
7.16
for details).

We prove Theorem
2.2
in Section
7
(that is, we prove its more precise formulation Theorem
7.12
) as an application of the abstract theory described in the next section. However, checking the conditions of the abstract theory entails a non-trivial amount of work, which is carried out in Section
7
.

2.3.
Abstract Framework and Central Limit Theorems

Consider a sequence of dynamical systems
f_{k}:M_{k}\to M_{k+1}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
4
4

4

We use the convention that
0\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
where
M_{k}
are compact Riemannian manifolds of uniform diameter, and the
f_{k}
are measurable functions with respect to the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Let
\mu_{k}
be a sequence of probability measures such that
(f_{k})_{*}\mu_{k}\ll\mu_{k+1}
.
For all
g\in L^{\infty}(M_{k},\mu_{k})
and
\varphi\in L^{1}(M_{k+1},\mu_{k+1})
we define the Transfer Operator
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
as

(2.3)

\int_{M_{k}}g\varphi\circ f_{k}\,d\mu_{k}=\int_{M_{k+1}}\varphi{\mathcal{L}}_{k}g\,d\mu_{k+1}.

A direct computation shows that,
\mu_{k+1}
-a.s., we have

{\mathcal{L}}_{k}g(x)=\sum_{y\in f_{k}^{-1}(x)}g(y)/J_{k}(y),

where
J_{k}(y)=\frac{d\mu_{k+1}(f_{k}y)}{d\mu_{k}(y)}
. Note that, by defintion,
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
is a positive operator and a contraction as an operator from
L^{1}(M_{k},\mu_{k})
to
L^{1}(M_{k+1},\mu_{k+1})
.
Next, assume that there exist convex cones
C_{k}\subset L^{1}(M_{k},\mu_{k})
,
C_{k}\cap-C_{k}=\emptyset
, with the following properties

(C-1)

for each
h\in C_{k}
we have
\int_{M_{k}}h\,d\mu_{k}>0
.

(C-2)

{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(C_{k})\subset C_{k+1}
and the diameter (with repect to the Hilbert metric, see
[
7
, Appendix D]
) of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(C_{k})
in
C_{k+1}
is uniformly bounded.

(C-3)

there exists
\mathbbm{e}_{k}\in C_{k}
such that for each
h\in V_{k}:=\operatorname{span}(C_{k})
there exists
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
:

\nu\mathbbm{e}_{k}+h\in C_{k}.

Without loss of generality, by (C-
1
), we can choose the
\mathbbm{e}_{k}
such that

(2.4)

\int_{M_{k}}\mathbbm{e}_{k}d\mu_{k}=1.

Remark 2.3
.

Note that the above setting is not the most general possible: e.g.
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
could be a more general transfer operator with some weight; condition (C-
2
) could be weakened to some diameter depending on
k
, provided that it does grow under appropriate control; in the conditions (O-
1
), (O-
2
) below one could let
K
grow, moderately, with
k
; and so on. We refrain from such endless generalizations, which the reader can easily work out, if needed, to present the basic idea in its more straightforward form.

Due to condition (C-
3
) we can associate to each
k
a Banach space
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
obtained by the completion of
V_{k}
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{k}
associated to the cone
C_{k}
:

(2.5)

\|h\|_{k}=\inf\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\;:\;-\lambda\mathbbm{e}_{k}\preceq h\preceq\lambda\mathbbm{e}_{k}\}

where
h\succeq g
iff
h-g\in C_{k}
(see
[
7
, Equation D.2.1]
for more details). By
[
7
, Lemma D.5]
it follows that
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
is a Banach lattice with the order structure
\succeq
. In particular, if
-g\preceq h\preceq g
, then
\|g\|_{k}\geq\|h\|_{k}
. Also
\|\mathbbm{e}_{k}\|_{k}=1
.

Next, note that if
h\succeq g
, i.e.
h-g\in C_{k}
, by condtion (C-
2
),
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(h-g)\in C_{k+1}
, so
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\succeq{\mathcal{L}}_{k}g
. That is,
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
is a positive, order preserving operator.
Accordingly, for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
we have
\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\alpha\|_{k+1}\leq\|\alpha\|_{k}\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\mathbbm{e}_{k}\|_{k+1}
.
5
5

5

In fact, by conditions (C-
2
) and (C-
3
),
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\mathbbm{e}_{k}\in V_{k+1}
so
\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\mathbbm{e}_{k}\|_{k+1}<\infty
.

In other words,
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\in L({\mathcal{B}}_{k},{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1})
. Moreover, by (C-
1
) we have

(2.6)

\left|\int_{M_{k}}\alpha d\mu_{k}\right|\leq\int_{M_{k}}\mathbbm{e}_{k}\|\alpha\|_{k}d\mu_{k}=\|\alpha\|_{k}.

In addition, by
[
7
, Lemma D.4]
(with the choice
\rho(g)=\int g
), there exists
c>0
such that, for each
k\geq j
,
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
, with
\int_{M_{j}}hd\mu_{j}=0
, we have

(2.7)

\left\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}h\right\|_{k+1}\leq 3e^{-c(k-j+1)}\|h\|_{j}.

We further assume

(C-4)

there exists
C_{*}>0
such that, for each
k\geq j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
, we have
\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}h\|_{k+1}\leq C_{*}\|h\|_{j}
.

Next we introduce the observables for which we shall prove a sequential Central Limit Theorem.
Let
\{g_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
g_{k}\in L^{1}(M_{k},\mu_{k})
be a real function such that

(O-1)

there exists
K>0
,
j_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
j_{0}>2
, such that, for each
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
, and
j\leq j_{0}
, we have

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(g_{k}^{j}h)\|_{k+1}\leq K^{j}\|h\|_{k}.

We define
f_{k,j}=f_{k-1}\circ\dots\circ f_{j}
,
f_{j,j}(x)=x
and consider the sum

S_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}g_{k}\circ f_{k,0}.

We let
x_{k}=g_{k}\circ f_{k,0}(x_{0})
, and assume that
x_{0}
is distributed according to a probability measure
d\nu_{0}=\rho d\mu_{0}
,

\rho\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
.
We will use the notation
{\mathbb{E}}
to denote the expectation with respect to the initial measure
d\nu_{0}=\rho d\mu_{0}
.

Then the
x_{k}
are random variables and

{\mathbb{E}}\left(S_{n}\right):=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{M_{0}}\rho\cdot g_{k}\circ f_{k,0}d\mu_{0}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{M_{k}}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho\;d\mu_{k}.

By (
2.6
), (O-
1
) and (C-
4
) we have

\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left|\int_{M_{k}}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho\;d\mu_{k}\right|=\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left|\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho d\mu_{k+1}\right|\leq KC_{*}\|\rho\|_{0}.

It is then natural to define

(2.8)

\hat{g}_{k}=g_{k}-\int_{M_{k}}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho d\mu_{k}

so that

(2.9)

\int_{M_{k}}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho d\mu_{k}=0,

and

(2.10)

\hat{S}_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}.

By definition,
{\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{S}_{n}\right)=0
. It is then natural to define

(2.11)

\sigma_{n}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}(\hat{S}_{n}^{2})=\sum_{k,j=0}^{n-1}\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho))\,d\mu_{n}.

Lemma 2.4
.

There exists
\tilde{C}>0
such that, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\sigma_{n}^{2}\leq\tilde{C}n.

Proof.

Indeed, using that
{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
, together with (
2.6
),
(C-
4
)
and (O-
1
), we may estimate

(2.12)

\begin{split}\sigma_{n}^{2}&=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}({\hat{g}}^{2}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho)\,d\mu_{k+1}\\
&\quad+2\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho))\,d\mu_{k+1}\\
&\leq nC_{*}K^{2}\|\rho\|_{0}+6C_{*}K^{2}\|\rho\|_{0}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}e^{-c(k-j-1)}\leq\tilde{C}n\,,\end{split}

where, in the last line, we have used (
2.7
) to estimate

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho)\|_{k}
, sicne we have that
\int_{M_{j+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho)\,d\mu_{j+1}=0
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j+1}
by condition (O-
1
).
∎

To continue, we need to complexify the Banach spaces
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
and extend the action of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
to such an extension (to simplify notation, we still call
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
the extension).

Lemma 2.5
.

There exists a canonical complex exension
{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
of
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
and the action of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
extends to a bounded operator in
L({\mathbb{B}}_{k},{\mathbb{B}}_{k+1})
.
Moreover, for each
k,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the norm of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{k}:{\mathbb{B}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{k+m+1}
is bounded by
\sqrt{2}C_{*}
.

The above fact is well known but, for the reader’s convenience, we provide the proof in Appendix
A
.

To keep the notation simple from now on we will use
\|\cdot\|_{k}
also for the norm on the complex space, since no confusion can arise.

Note that if
f,g\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
then, by (
2.6
),

(2.13)

\left|\int_{M_{k}}(f+ig)d\mu_{k}\right|\leq\left|\int_{M_{k}}fd\mu_{k}\right|+\left|\int_{M_{k}}gd\mu_{k}\right|\leq\|f\|_{k}+\|g\|_{k}\leq\sqrt{2}\|f+ig\|_{k}.

Next, for
k,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\leq n
, and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}
: for each
h\in{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
,

(2.14)

{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}h={\mathcal{L}}_{k}(e^{i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\hat{g}_{k}}h).

Note that, for
h\in L^{1}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have
e^{i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda g_{k}}h\in L^{1}
, so
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}
is also well defined, and a contraction, as an operator in
L^{1}(M_{k},{\mathbb{C}})
.
In addition, we assume

(O-2)

There exists
\lambda_{0}>0
and
K>0
, such that for all

j,k,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
j\leq k\leq n
,

|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{0}\sigma_{n}
, and
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
we have

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,\lambda,n}h\|_{k+1}\leq K\|h\|_{j}
.

Finally, we state a stronger assumption (the composition of twisted transfer operators is of Perron-Frobenius type), that will allow us to obtain stronger results:

(O-3)

There exists
\lambda_{0},c,K>0
and elements
h_{k,j,\lambda}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
,
\ell_{k,j,\lambda}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}^{\prime}
,
k,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that, for all
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{0}\sigma_{n}
and
k,j,l,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\int_{M_{k}}h_{k,j,\lambda}d\mu_{k}=\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(\mathbbm{e}_{j})=1
,
\|h_{k,j,\lambda}\|_{k}+\|\ell_{k,j,\lambda}\|_{j}^{\prime}\leq K
,
6
6

6

Where, for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{j}
, we have
\|\ell\|_{j}^{\prime}=\sup_{\|h\|_{j}\leq 1}|\ell(h)|
.

|\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(h_{j,l,\lambda})|\geq K^{-1}
. Morever, there exist
\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|\leq K
such that for all
j,k,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
j\leq k\leq n
, and
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
we have

(2.15)

\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,\lambda,n}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,\lambda,n}h-\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}h_{k,j,\lambda}\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(h)\|_{k}\leq K|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|e^{-c(k-j)}\|h\|_{j}.

Notation
.

In the following, we will use
c_{\#},C_{\#}
for a generic constant depending only on the constants in Conditions (C-1)–(C-4) and (O-
1
)–(O-
3
). Also, given a Banach space
{\mathcal{B}}
, we will use the notation
{\mathcal{O}}(a)
,
a\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, to stand for an arbitrary element of
h\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
\|h\|\leq C_{\#}a
. We will not specify explicitly the Banach space (which could be
{\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}},{\mathcal{B}}_{k},{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
etc …) since it will always be clear from the context.

Our main results are the following.

Theorem 2.6
.

If the conditions (C-1),(C-2),(C-3), (C-4) and (O-
1
), with
j_{0}=3
, and (O-
2
) are satisfied and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma_{n}=\infty
, then for each
\varpi>3
there exists
C_{\varpi},\lambda_{1}>0
such that, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{1}\sigma_{n}
, we have

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}}\right)-e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}\right|\leq C_{\varpi}\left(\frac{(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}\lambda^{4}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}+\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}+n\frac{\lambda^{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}}\right).

We will see that the above result yields helpful information only if for each
C_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
there exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sigma_{n}\geq C_{0}n^{\frac{1}{3}}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
.

Theorem 2.7
.

If the conditions (C-1),(C-2),(C-3), (C-4) and (O-
1
), with
j_{0}=3
, together with hypothesis (O-
3
) are satisfied, then for each
\varpi>3
there exist constants
C_{\varpi},\lambda_{1}>0
and an analytic functions
A_{n}
such that, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{1}\sigma_{n}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{-1}
,
we have

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}}\right)=e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+A_{n}(\lambda)}\\
&\left|A_{n}(\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\varpi}n\left[\lambda^{3}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}|\lambda|\right]\\
&\left|A_{n}^{\prime}(\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\varpi}n\left[\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right].\end{split}

The proof of Theorem
2.6
is the content of Section
3
, while
Theorem
2.7
is proven in Section
4
.

To compare with other results, let us compute what the above results imply for the distribution function

(2.16)

F_{n}(x)={\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\frac{\hat{S}_{n}}{\sigma_{n}}\leq x\right\}\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbbm{1}}_{\{\hat{S}_{n}/\sigma_{n}\leq x\}}\right).

Our first result using (O-
2
) is nontrivial only if
\sigma_{n}\gg n^{\frac{1}{3}}
. This is the same restriction in
[
21
]
. But there, the error is smaller

Corollary 2.8
.

If the conditions (C-1),(C-2),(C-3), (C-4) and (O-
1
), with
j_{0}=3
, together with hypothesis (O-
2
) are satisfied,
then for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

(2.17)

\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}dy\right|\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\frac{3}{4}}n^{\frac{1}{4}},

Proof.

By
[
18
, equation (3.13) of Chapter XVI.3]
, Theorem
2.6
implies

\begin{split}\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}dy\right|&\leq\frac{C_{\varpi}}{\pi}\int_{-T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\left[\frac{(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}\zeta^{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}+n\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\zeta\right]d\zeta+\frac{24}{\pi T_{n}}\\
&\leq C_{\#}C_{\varpi}\left[\frac{(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}T_{n}^{4}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}+n\frac{T_{n}^{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}T_{n}\right]+\frac{24}{\pi T_{n}},\end{split}

for each sequence
\{T_{n}\}
. Choosing
T_{n}=\sigma_{n}^{\frac{3}{4}}n^{-\frac{1}{4}}
, and recalling that (
2.12
) implies
\sigma_{n}\leq C_{\#}\sqrt{n}
, the result follows.
∎

To obtain a stronger result, we must assume (O-
3
) and apply Theorem
2.7
.

Corollary 2.9
.

If the conditions (C-1),(C-2),(C-3), (C-4) and (O-
1
), with
j_{0}=3
, together with hypothesis (O-
3
) are satisfied, then for each
c_{\star}\in(0,1)
and for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\sigma_{n}\geq\max\{1,c_{\star}n^{\frac{1}{3}}\ln(n+1)\}
, there exists
\bar{C}>0
such that

(2.18)

\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}dy\right|\leq\bar{C}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}n.

Proof.

We use again
[
18
, equation (3.13) of Chapter XVI.3]
to write

\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}dy\right|\leq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-T_{n}}^{T_{n}}\left|\frac{e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}-e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}+A_{n}(\zeta)}}{\zeta}\right|d\zeta+\frac{24}{\pi T_{n}}.

We make the choice
T_{n}=\frac{\sigma_{n}^{3}}{C_{\star}n(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}}
, for some constant
C_{\star}>0
large enough, and
\varpi=4
. By Theorem
2.7
it follows that

\begin{split}&\frac{\zeta^{2}}{4}\geq C_{\varpi}n\left|\zeta^{3}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}\right|\\
&\left|\frac{d}{d\zeta}e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}+A_{n}(\zeta)}\right|\leq C_{0},\end{split}

for all
|\zeta|\leq T_{n}
.
Also, setting
S_{n}=\frac{\sigma_{n}}{n^{\frac{1}{3}}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{\frac{2}{3}}}
, we have that
|A_{n}(\zeta)|
is uniformly bounded for all
|\zeta|\leq S_{n}
. Hence, for all
|\zeta|\leq S_{n}
we have, by Theorem
2.7
,

\begin{split}\left|\frac{1-e^{A_{n}(\zeta)}}{\zeta}\right|&\leq C_{\#}\left|\frac{\int_{0}^{\zeta}A^{\prime}(z)dz}{\zeta}\right|\leq C_{\#}\sup_{z\in[0,\zeta]}|A^{\prime}(z)|\\
&\leq C_{\#}C_{\varpi}n\left|\zeta^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right|.\end{split}

Accordingly,

\begin{split}\left|F_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{-\frac{y^{2}}{2}}dy\right|\leq&\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-S_{n}}^{S_{n}}e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2}}C_{\varpi}n\left[\zeta^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-4}\right]d\zeta\\
&+\frac{2e^{C_{\varpi}c_{\star}^{-\varpi}}}{\pi}\int_{S_{n}}^{T_{n}}e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{4}}+\frac{24}{\pi T_{n}}\\
&\leq\bar{C}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}n,\end{split}

for some
\bar{C}
large enough.
∎

We conclude with a brief discussion on the relation with previous results.

Remark 2.10
.

The above results are only slightly weaker than
[
21
]
. However, they apply to a much wider range of systems. In particular, they are tailored for possible applications to the problem of a random Lorenz gas where the observable may not the bounded (e.g., see the relevant observables in the recent
[
15
]
). For bounded observables, one can take advantage of the fact that

{\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}}\right)

is an analytic function of
\lambda
. More generally, our results can be improved by following the same strategy used here and simply computing more terms in the various Taylor expansions.
To obtain optimal results, it is however necessary to have bounds on the higher moments in terms of the variance. The latter strategy is used in the recent paper
[
14
]
, where much stronger results are obtained but limited to bounded observables and expanding maps. Unfortunately, it is not obvious how to extend the martingale approximation argument used to control the higher momenta in
[
14
]
to the present general setting.

3.
Proof of Theorem
2.6

In order to compute

(3.1)

\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)={\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}}\right)

we show that it satisfies a differential inequality.

Proposition 3.1
.

For each
\varpi>0
, there exist
C_{\varpi},\lambda_{1}>0
such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{1}\sigma_{n}
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=-\lambda\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda)\\
&|{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda)|\leq C_{\varpi}\left(\frac{(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}|\lambda|^{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}+|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}+n\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}}\right).\end{split}

Proposition
3.1
immediately implies Theorem
2.6
.

Proof of Theorem
2.6
using Proposition
3.1
.

Setting
\theta_{n}(\lambda)=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)
, we compute

\theta_{n}^{\prime}(\lambda)=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda).

Since
\Upsilon_{n}(0)=1
, we have
\theta_{n}(0)=1
, hence

\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}+\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}-\xi^{2}}{2}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\xi)d\xi.

Moreover, using that
e^{-(\lambda^{2}-\xi^{2})/2}\leq 1
, we have

\left|\int_{0}^{\lambda}e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}-\xi^{2}}{2}}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\xi)d\xi\right|\leq\sup_{|\xi|\leq|\lambda|}{|{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\xi)|}\,|\lambda|\leq C_{\varpi}\left(\frac{(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}\lambda^{4}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}+\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}+n\frac{|\lambda|^{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}}\right).

∎

For the remainder of this section, we fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and prove Proposition
3.1
. Since
n
is fixed, to simplify
notation a little, we will drop the last index
n
in the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}
from (
2.14
). That is,

(3.2)

{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}h:={\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}h={\mathcal{L}}_{k}(e^{i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda{\hat{g}_{k}}}h)\,.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

For
\mu_{0}
-almost every
x
we have

\frac{d}{d\lambda}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}(x)}=i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}(x)e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}(x)}.

By the Schwartz inequality, recalling (
2.6
) and Conditions (O-
1
) and (C-
4
), we have

(3.3)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}(|\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}(x)|)&\leq\left[\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\left[(\hat{g}_{k})^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho\right]d\mu_{k+1}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&\leq KC_{*}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\rho\|_{0}<\infty.\end{split}

Accordingly,

(3.4)

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}_{n}}\right).

For some constant
C_{L}>0
, to be chosen later large enough, we define

(3.5)

L_{n}=C_{L}\ln\sigma_{n}.

Next, for each
k\leq n
let

\begin{split}&\hat{S}^{i}_{n,k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}\\
&\hat{S}^{e}_{n,k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-k|>L_{n}\end{subarray}}\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}.\end{split}

Also, we define

\begin{split}&\Theta^{0}_{k}(\xi,\lambda)={\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{e}_{n,k}+i\xi\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{i}_{n,k}}\right)\\
&\Theta^{1}_{k}(\xi,\lambda)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{e}_{n,k}+i\xi\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{i}_{n,k}}\right)\\
&\Theta^{2}_{k,j}(\xi,\lambda)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{e}_{n,k}+i\xi\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{i}_{n,k}}\right)\\
&\Theta^{3}_{k,j,l}(\xi,\lambda)={\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k,0}\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}\hat{g}_{l}\circ f_{l,0}e^{i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{e}_{n,k}+i\xi\sigma_{n}^{-1}\hat{S}^{i}_{n,k}}\right).\end{split}

Lemma 3.2
.

For each
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the functions
\Theta^{i}(\xi,\cdot)
are
{\mathcal{C}}^{j_{*}-i}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}})
.

Proof.

The computation is the same as for equation (
3.3
), using (
2.6
) and Conditions (O-
1
) and (C-
4
).
∎

By (
3.4
) and Lemma
3.2
we can Taylor expand with respect to
\xi
and write

(3.6)

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=&i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Theta^{1}_{k}(\lambda,\lambda)\\
=&i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\Theta^{1}_{k}(0,\lambda)-\sigma_{n}^{-2}\lambda\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\Theta^{2}_{k,j}(0,\lambda)\\
&-i\sigma_{n}^{-3}\int_{0}^{\lambda}d\xi\int_{0}^{\xi}dz\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|l-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\Theta^{3}_{k,j,l}(z,\lambda).\end{split}

Remark 3.3
.

Note that nothing prevents us from expanding
\Theta^{1}_{k}
to higher orders. Doing so yields better estimates, but at the price of a much lengthier computation. We refrain from doing so as our goal is to present clearly the idea rather than to state the optimal results.

Next, we must compute the terms in the above equation. To this end, we use the twisted transfer operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}
(recall (
3.2
)).
Let us define, for all
k,j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(3.7)

{\mathcal{L}}_{k,j,\lambda}=\begin{cases}{\mathbbm{1}}&\textrm{ if }k<0\\
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{\max\{j,0\},\lambda}&\textrm{ if }k\geq j\\
{\mathbbm{1}}&\textrm{ if }k<j.\end{cases}

Note that, for
h\in L^{1}
,

\int_{M_{k+1}}\left|{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}h\right|\leq\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\left|h\right|=\int_{M_{k}}|h|.

Hence, by (
2.13
), (O-
1
) and (C-
4
), we have

\left|\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,\lambda}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,\lambda}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0,\lambda}\rho\right|\leq\sqrt{2}KC_{*}^{2}\|\rho\|_{0}.

Note that, for all
L_{n}\leq k\leq n-L_{n}
and
k\leq j\leq k+L_{n}

(3.8)

\begin{split}&\Theta^{0}_{k}(\xi,\lambda)=\int_{M_{n}}\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n}+1,\xi}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n},0,\lambda}\rho\\
&\Theta^{1}_{k}(\xi,\lambda)=\int_{M_{n}}\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\xi}\,\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,\xi}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n},0,\lambda}\rho\\
&\Theta^{2}_{k,j}(\xi,\lambda)=\int_{M_{n}}\!\!\!\!\!\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},j,\xi}\,\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,\xi}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,\xi}\,{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n},0,\lambda}\rho.\\
\end{split}

The above formulae correspond to the case
j\geq k
, the definition in the other cases being obvious.
To estimate the above terms, the following lemmata will be instrumental.

Lemma 3.4
.

For each
k,m_{1},m_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
h\in{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
, we have

\begin{split}&\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{1}+m_{2},k+m_{2},0}\,\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h\|_{k+m_{1}+m_{2}+1}\leq C_{\#}e^{-c\min\{m_{1},m_{2}\}}\|h\|_{k}\\
&\left|\int_{M_{k+m_{1}+m_{2}}}\hskip-12.0pt{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{1}+m_{2},k+m_{2},0}\,\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h\right|\leq C_{\#}e^{-cm_{2}}\|h\|_{k}\\
&\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{1},k,0}\,\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho\|_{k+m_{1}+1}\leq C_{\#}e^{-cm_{1}}\end{split}

Proof.

Let us define

(3.9)

\phi_{k,j}:={\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,0}\mathbbm{e}_{j}.

By (C-
4
),
\|\phi_{k,j}\|_{k}\leq C_{*}
while (
2.3
) implies
\int_{M_{k}}\phi_{k,j}d\mu_{k}=1
.
Equation (
2.7
) yields

(3.10)

\begin{split}\left\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,0}h-\phi_{k,j}\int_{M_{j}}hd\mu_{j}\right\|_{k}&=\left\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,0}\left[h-\mathbbm{e}_{j}\int_{M_{j}}hd\mu_{j}\right]\right\|_{k}\\
&\leq C_{\#}e^{-c(k-j)}\|h\|_{j}.\end{split}

In particular, choosing
h={\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,0}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0,0}\mathbbm{e}_{0}
,

(3.11)

\left\|\phi_{k,0}-\phi_{k,j}\right\|_{k}\leq C_{\#}e^{-c(k-j)}.

Then, using (
3.10
) multiple times, conditions (O-
1
), (C-
4
) and equation (
3.11
) yields

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{1}+m_{2},k+m_{2},0}\,\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h=\phi_{k+m_{1}+m_{2}+1,k+m_{2}+1}\\
&\times\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2},0}\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-cm_{1}})\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2},0}\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}\phi_{k+m_{2},k}\int h\|_{k+m_{2}}\\
&=\phi_{k+m_{1}+m_{2}+1,k+m_{2}+1}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2},0}\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-cm_{1}}+e^{-cm_{2}})\|h\|_{k}\\
&=\phi_{k+m_{1}+m_{2}+1,0}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2},0}\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-cm_{1}}+e^{-cm_{2}})\|h\|_{k}\\
&=\phi_{k+m_{1}+m_{2},0}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2},0}\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,0,0}\rho\int_{M_{k}}h+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-cm_{1}}+e^{-cm_{2}})\|h\|_{k}\\
&=\phi_{k+m_{1}+m_{2},0}\int\hat{g}_{k+m_{2}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,0,0}\rho\int_{M_{k}}h+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-cm_{1}}+e^{-cm_{2}})\|h\|_{k}\\
&={\mathcal{O}}(e^{-c\min\{m_{1},m_{2}\}})\|h\|_{k},\end{split}

where, in the third equality, we have used again (
3.10
) to estimate

\begin{split}&\left\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}h-{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,0,0}\rho\int_{M_{k}}h\right\|_{k+m_{2}}=\left\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+m_{2}-1,k,0}\left(h-{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho\int_{M_{k}}h\right)\right\|_{k+m_{2}}\\
&\leq\left\|\phi_{k+m_{2},k}\int\left(h-{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho\int_{M_{k}}h\right)\right\|_{k+m_{2}}+C_{\#}e^{-cm_{2}}\left\|h-{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho\int_{M_{k}}h\right\|_{k}\\
&\leq C_{\#}e^{-cm_{2}}\|h\|_{k}.\end{split}

This proves the first inequality. The other two are proven similarly.
∎

Let

(3.12)

\begin{split}&\Gamma^{0}_{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n}+1,0}\\
&\Gamma^{1}_{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,0}\,\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,0}\\
&\Gamma^{2}_{k,j}={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},j,0}\,\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,0}.\\
\end{split}

Lemma 3.5
.

For each
\varpi>0
there exists
C_{L}=C_{L}(\varpi)>0
such that for all
n-L_{n}\geq k\geq L_{n}
and
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k-L_{n}}
, we have

\begin{split}&\|\Gamma^{1}_{k}h\|_{k+L_{n}}\leq C_{\#}\sigma^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}}\\
&\|\Gamma^{2}_{k,j}h-\Sigma_{k,j}\Gamma_{k}^{0}h\|_{k+L_{n}}\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}},\end{split}

where

(3.13)

\Sigma_{k,j}=\int\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,0}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,0,0}\rho.

Proof.

Equations (
2.7
), (
2.6
) and conditions (C-
4
), (O-
1
) imply

\begin{split}\left\|\Gamma^{1}_{k}h\right\|_{k+L_{n}}&\leq\left\|\phi_{k+L_{n},k+1}\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n}+1},0}hd\mu_{k}\right\|_{k+1}+C_{\#}e^{-cL_{n}}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}}\\
&\leq C_{\#}\left|\int_{M_{k}}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho d\mu_{0}\right|\cdot\left|\int_{M_{k-L_{n}}}hd\mu_{k-L_{n}}\right|+C_{\#}K\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}}\\
&\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}}\end{split}

by (
2.9
) and provided we have chosen
C_{L}
large enough. Next, we study
\Gamma^{2}_{k,j}
for
j\geq k
, the case
j<k
being identical. If
|k-j|\geq L_{n}/2
, then

(3.14)

\begin{split}&\Gamma^{2}_{k,j}h={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},j,0}\,\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,0}h\\
&={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},j,0}\,\hat{g}_{j}\phi_{j,k+1}\int_{M_{k+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,0}h+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-c|k-j|}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}})\\
&={\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}})\end{split}

provided we choose
C_{L}
large enough. On the other hand, if
|k-j|\leq L_{n}/2
, then
k+L_{n}-j\geq L_{n}/2
and
j-k+L_{n}\geq L_{n}/2
, hence, recalling (
3.10
),

(3.15)

\begin{split}\Gamma^{2}_{k,j}h=&\phi_{k+L_{n},j+1}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n}+1,0}h\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-c|k+L_{n}-j|}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}})\\
&=\phi_{k+L_{n},j+1}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho\int h+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}})\\
&=\Sigma_{k,j}\Gamma_{k}^{0}h+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}}).\end{split}

The Lemma follows noting that, for
|k-j|\geq L_{n}/2
,
|\Sigma_{k,j}|\leq C_{\#}\sigma^{-\varpi}
.
∎

We can now estimate the terms in (
3.6
) one at a time.

If
L_{n}\leq k\leq n-L_{n}
, Lemma (
3.5
) implies

\left|\Theta^{1}_{k}(0,\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\\

If
k\geq n-L_{n}
, then by the second inequality of Lemma
3.4
and condition (O-
2
)

\left|\Theta^{1}_{k}(0,\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n},0,\lambda}\rho\|_{k-L_{n}}\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}.

While, if
k\leq L_{n}
, then by the equation (
2.6
), condition (O-
2
) and third inequality of Lemma
3.4

\left|\Theta^{1}_{k}(0,\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\#}\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,0}\,\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,0}\rho\|_{k+L_{n}+1}\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}.

It follows that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.16)

|\Theta^{1}_{k}(0,\lambda)|\leq C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}.

Next, let us compute
\Theta^{2}_{k,j}(0,\lambda)
. As before we treat only the case
j\geq k
since the case
j<k
is identical and is left to the reader.

For
n-L_{n}\geq k\geq L_{n}
Lemma
3.5
implies

(3.17)

\Theta^{2}_{k,j}(0,\lambda)=\Sigma_{k,j}\Theta^{0}_{k}(0,\lambda)+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}).

Note that, by equations (
2.11
) and (
3.10
),

(3.18)

\sigma_{n}^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{|j-k|\leq L_{n}}\Sigma_{k,j}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}).

The other possibilities can be treated as we did for
\Theta^{1}_{k}
and yield a contribution of order
\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}
.
Finally, we compute
7
7

7

Note that
\int_{M_{0}}|\hat{g}_{l}|^{2}\circ f_{l,0}\rho\,d\mu_{0}=\int_{M_{l}}|\hat{g}_{l}|^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{l,0}\rho\,d\mu_{l}=\int_{M_{l+1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{l,0}|\hat{g}_{l}|^{2}{\mathcal{L}}_{l,0,0}\rho\,d\mu_{l+1}
, so equation (
2.6
) and conditions (O-
1
), (C-
4
) imply that
\hat{g}_{l}\circ f_{l,0}\in L^{2}(M_{0},\nu_{0})
.

(3.19)

\begin{split}&\left|\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,l\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-k|\leq L_{n}\\
|l-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\Theta^{3}_{k,j,l}(z,\lambda)\right|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}K{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|l-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\hat{g}_{l}\circ f_{l,0}\right]^{2}\right)\\
&\phantom{\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}}\leq K\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|l-k|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}\hat{g}_{l}\circ f_{l,0}\right)\\
&\phantom{\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}}\leq K\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|j-l|\leq 2L_{n}\end{subarray}}(2L_{n}-|l-j|){\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}\hat{g}_{l}\circ f_{l,0}\right)\\
&\phantom{\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}}\leq 2K\sigma_{n}^{2}L_{n}-K\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\\
|s|\leq 2L_{n}\end{subarray}}|s|{\mathbb{E}}\left(\hat{g}_{j}\circ f_{j,0}\hat{g}_{j-s}\circ f_{j-s,0}\right)\\
&\phantom{\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}}\leq 2K\sigma_{n}^{2}L_{n}+C_{\#}n\end{split}

since the sum over
s
is bounded by (
3.10
).

To conclude, note that

(3.20)

\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=\Theta^{0}_{k}(\lambda,\lambda)=\Theta^{0}_{k}(0,\lambda)+\partial_{\xi}\Theta^{0}_{k}(0,\lambda)\lambda+\int_{0}^{\lambda}d\eta\int_{0}^{\eta}dz\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Theta^{0}_{k}(z,\lambda).

Arguing as before, we have

(3.21)

\begin{split}&|\partial_{\xi}\Theta^{0}_{k}(0,\lambda)|\leq C\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\\
&|\partial_{\xi}^{2}\Theta^{0}_{k}(0,\lambda)|\leq C_{\#}C_{L}^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}.\end{split}

Hence, we can rewrite (
3.17
) as

(3.22)

\Theta^{2}_{k,j}(0,\lambda)=\Sigma_{k,j}\Upsilon_{n}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Sigma_{k,j}\sigma_{n}^{-2}C_{L}^{2}\lambda^{2}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right).

Collecting the above computations, we have

\begin{split}\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)&=-\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,\cdots,n-1\}\\
|k-j|\leq L_{n}\end{subarray}}\left[\Sigma_{k,j}\lambda\Upsilon(\lambda)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Sigma_{k,j}\sigma_{n}^{-2}\lambda^{3}C_{L}^{2}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\lambda\right)\right]\\
&\phantom{=}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(n\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-3}+\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}C_{L}\ln\sigma_{n}\right)\\
&=-\lambda\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{C_{L}^{2}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}\lambda^{3}}{\sigma_{n}^{2}}+\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}+n\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\sigma_{n}^{3}}\right),\end{split}

and this concludes the proof recalling that
C_{L}
is a constant depending on
\varpi
, see Lemma
3.5
.
∎

4.
Proof of Theorem
2.7

We define
\Upsilon_{n}
as in equation (
3.1
).

Proposition 4.1
.

For each
\varpi>0
, there exists
\lambda_{1}>0
such that, for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{1}\sigma_{n}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{-1}
, we have

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=-\left(\lambda+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda)\right)\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)\\
&\Upsilon(0)=1\\
&\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\varpi}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-3}(\ln\sigma_{n})^{2}n+n\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right).\end{split}

Proposition
4.1
immediately implies Theorem
2.7
.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

Let
A_{n}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\xi)d\xi
then Proposition
4.1
implies
\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}-A_{n}(\lambda)}
.
∎

To conclude we must thus prove Proposition
4.1
.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We start again our proof by

(4.1)

\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho d\mu_{n},

where we have used the same notation as in (
3.8
).
If
2L_{n}\leq k\leq n-2L_{n}
, then, recalling conditions (O-
2
) and (O-
3
) we can write

(4.2)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}&\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho=\alpha_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}\alpha_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}h_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}\\
&\times\ell_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda})\ell_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}(\rho)\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{-\varpi}\alpha_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}\alpha_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda})\end{split}

provided we have chosen
C_{L}
large enough.

Lemma 4.2
.

There exists
n_{1}\in{\mathbb{N}}
each
n\geq k>j>l
,
k-l\geq n_{1}
, we have

\alpha_{k,l,\lambda}=\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\alpha_{j-1,l,\lambda}\ell_{k,j,\lambda}\left(h_{j,l,\lambda}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\alpha_{k,l,\lambda}\alpha_{j-1,l,\lambda}e^{-c\min\{|k-j|,|j-l|\}}\right).

Proof.

By assumption (O-
3
) we have, on the one hand

\left|\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,l,\lambda}(\mathbbm{e}_{l})d\mu_{k}-\alpha_{k,l,\lambda}\right|\leq K|\alpha_{k,l,\lambda}|e^{-c|k-l|}.

On the other hand

\begin{split}\int_{M_{k}}&{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,l,\lambda}(\mathbbm{e}_{l})d\mu_{k}=\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,l,\lambda}(\mathbbm{e}_{l})\right)d\mu_{k}\\
&=\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\ell_{k,j,\lambda}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,l,\lambda}(\mathbbm{e}_{l})\right)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}e^{-c|k-j|}\left\|{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,l,\lambda}(\mathbbm{e}_{l})\right\|_{j}\right)\\
&=\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\alpha_{j-1,l,\lambda}\ell_{k,j,\lambda}\left(h_{j,l,\lambda}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\alpha_{j-1,l,\lambda}e^{-c\min\{|k-j|,|j-l|\}}\right).\end{split}

Hence, If
Ke^{-cn_{1}}\leq 1/2
, the lemma follows.
∎

Since

(4.3)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{l,j,\lambda}h=&{\mathcal{L}}_{l,j,0}h+i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{m=j}^{l}{\mathcal{L}}_{j,m,0}\,\hat{g}_{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{m-1,j,0}h\\
&-\sigma_{n}^{-2}\sum_{m,s=j}^{l}\int_{0}^{\lambda}(\lambda-\xi){\mathcal{L}}_{l,m,\xi}\,\hat{g}_{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{m-1,s,\xi}\hat{g}_{s}{\mathcal{L}}_{s-1,l,\xi}hd\xi,\end{split}

we can compute

(4.4)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\lambda}&\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},\lambda}h={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},0}h\\
&+i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},j,0}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,0}\,\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},0}h\\
&-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\sigma_{n}^{2}}\sum_{m,s=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},m,0}\,\hat{g}_{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{m-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,s,0}\hat{g}_{s}{\mathcal{L}}_{s-1,k-L_{n},0}h\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{3}\sigma_{n}^{-3}L_{n}^{3}\|h\|_{k-L_{n}}).\end{split}

Lemma 4.3
.

For
\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}\sigma_{n}
, we have
8
8

8

Recall that
\Sigma_{k,j}
is defiend in (
3.13
), while
\lambda_{0}
refers to condition (O-
3
).

\begin{split}\Big\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}&-i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\!\!\!\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}\!\!\!\Sigma_{k,j}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\Big\|\leq\\
&\leq C_{\#}\left[\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right].\end{split}

Proof.

Equation (
4.4
)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}&{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}={\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},0}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\\
&+i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{j=k}^{k+L_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},j,0}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},0}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\\
&+i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,0}\hat{g}_{j}{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1,k-L_{n},0}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(\left[\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}\right]\|h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\|_{k-L_{n}}).\end{split}

By Lemma
3.5
and equation (
4.3
) it follows

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}=&i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}\Sigma_{k,j}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n},0}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(\left[\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right]\|h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\|_{k-L_{n}})\\
=&i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}\Sigma_{k,j}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(\left[\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right]\|h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\|_{k-L_{n}}).\end{split}

The Lemma follows recalling that, by condition (O-
3
),
\|h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\|_{k-L_{n}}\leq K
.
∎

To continue, we need some rough estimates.

Lemma 4.4
.

There exists
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda_{0})
such that, for all
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{1}\sigma_{n}L_{n}^{-1}
and
j\leq k\leq n
,
k-j\leq L_{n}
, we have, for each
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
,

a)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|\geq\frac{1}{2(1+K)}

b)

\displaystyle|\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(h)|\geq c_{\#}\left|\int_{M_{j}}h\right|-C_{\#}\left[\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}|k-j|+e^{-c|k-j|}\right]\|h\|_{j}

c)

\displaystyle\|h_{k,j,\lambda}-\phi_{k,j}\|_{k}\leq C_{\#}\left[\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}|k-j|+e^{-c|k-j|}\right].

Proof.

By condition (O-
3
) we have

\left|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}-\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{j}d\mu_{k}\right|\leq Ke^{-c|k-j|}|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|.

While (
4.3
) and (
2.4
) and Condition (O-
1
) imply

\begin{split}\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{j}=&\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,0}\mathbbm{e}_{j}+i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sum_{m=j}^{k}\int_{0}^{\lambda}d\xi\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,m,\xi}\,\hat{g}_{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{m-1,j,\xi}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\\
=&1+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda|k-j|).\end{split}

Which yields

(4.5)

|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|\geq\frac{1-C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda|k-j|}{1+Ke^{-c|k-j|}}.

The above implies
|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|\geq(2+2K)^{-1}
, provided
|k-j|\leq L_{n}
and
\lambda_{1}
is chosen small enough.
Next, for each
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
,

\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}h=\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(h)+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-c|k-j|}\alpha_{k,l,\lambda}\|h\|_{j}).

Thus, for
|k-j|\leq L_{n}
, by the first statement of the Lemma and equation (
4.3
)

(4.6)

\begin{split}|\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(h)|&\geq c_{\#}\left|\int_{M_{k}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}h\right|-C_{\#}e^{-c|k-j|}\|h\|_{j}\\
&\geq c_{\#}\left|\int_{M_{j}}h\right|-C_{\#}\left[\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}|k-j|+e^{-c|k-j|}\right]\|h\|_{j},\end{split}

proving item (b).

Finally, by Conditions (O-
3
), (O-
2
) and equation (
4.3
) we have

\begin{split}&\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}\left(h_{k,j,\lambda}-\phi_{k,j}\right)={\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{j}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,0}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,j,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}e^{-c|k-j|}\right)\\
&=\sum_{l=j}^{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,l+1,0}{\mathcal{L}}_{l,j,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,l+1,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{l+1}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,l,0}{\mathcal{L}}_{l-1,j,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,l,\lambda}\mathbbm{e}_{l}\\
&\phantom{=}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}e^{-c|k-j|}\right)={\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}|k-j|+\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}e^{-c|k-j|}\right).\end{split}

The Lemma follows remembering (
4.5
).
∎

Using Lemma
4.3
in equation (
4.2
) and recalling Lemma
4.2
we obtain, for
2L_{n}\leq k\leq n-2L_{n}
, choosing
C_{L}
large enough,

(4.7)

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho=i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\!\!\!\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}\!\!\!\Sigma_{k,j}\alpha_{n,k+L_{n}+1}\alpha_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}h_{n,k+L_{n}+1,\lambda}\\
&\times\ell_{n,k+L_{n}+1}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\right)\left[1+{\mathcal{O}}(\left[\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}L_{n}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right])\right]\\
&\times\ell_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}(\rho)+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma^{-\varpi}\alpha_{n,0,\lambda})\\
&=\left(i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\!\!\!\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}\Sigma_{k,j}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right)\right){\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho\\
&\phantom{=}+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\alpha_{n,0,\lambda}),\end{split}

where, in the second line, we have used the fact that Lemma
4.4
implies, recalling Condition (O-
3
),

\begin{split}\left|\ell_{n,k+L_{n},\lambda}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+L_{n}-1,k-L_{n},\lambda}h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda})\right|\geq&\left|\alpha_{k+L_{n},k-L_{n},\lambda}\ell_{n,k+L_{n},\lambda}(h_{k+L_{n},0,\lambda})\right|\\
&\times\left|\ell_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}(h_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda})\right|-C_{\#}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi})\\
\geq&c_{\#}-C_{\#}\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\geq c_{\#}.\end{split}

To complete the estimate needed for Proposition
4.1
, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

If
C_{L}
is choosen large enough, there exists
c_{\#}>0
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{1}\sigma_{n}L_{n}^{-1}
, we have

\left|\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho\right|\geq c_{\#}|\alpha_{n,0,\lambda}|\,.

We postpone the proof of the lemma and use it to complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Applying Lemma
4.5
to (
4.7
), we obtain

(4.8)

\begin{split}\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho=&\left(i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\!\!\!\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{k+L_{n}}\Sigma_{k,j}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right)\right)\\
&\times\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho\,,\end{split}

which is our main estimate in the case
2L_{n}\leq k\leq n-2L_{n}
.

It remains to consider the cases
k\leq 2L_{n}
and
k\geq n-2L_{n}
. If
k>n-2L_{n}
, then, using (
4.3
),

\begin{split}&\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho d\mu_{n}=\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},0}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\rho d\mu_{n}\\
&+i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda\sum_{m=k-L_{n}}^{n}\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,m,0}\,\hat{g}_{m}{\mathcal{L}}_{m-1,k,0}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,k-L_{n},0}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\rho d\mu_{n}\\
&+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-2}\lambda^{2}L_{n}^{2}\alpha_{n,0,\lambda}).\end{split}

Arguing as in Lemma
4.3
yields

(4.9)

\begin{split}\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}&\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho=\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{n}\left(i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\Sigma_{k,j}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}\right)\right)\\
&\times\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho+{\mathcal{O}}(\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\alpha_{n,0,\lambda})\\
&=\sum_{j=k-L_{n}}^{n}\left(i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\Sigma_{k,j}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right)\right)\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho,\end{split}

where we have applied Lemma
4.5
in the last line.

Analogously, if
k\leq 2L_{n}
, then

(4.10)

\begin{split}\int_{M_{n}}\hskip-10.0pt{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,k,\lambda}\hat{g}_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1,0,\lambda}\rho=&\sum_{j=0}^{k+L_{n}}\left(i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\Sigma_{k,j}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-2}L_{n}^{2}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right)\right)\\
&\times\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho.\end{split}

Substituting respectively (
4.8
), (
4.9
) or (
4.10
)
in each case in equation (
4.1
), summing over
k
, and recalling (
3.18
) we finally have

\begin{split}&\frac{d}{d\lambda}\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)=-\left(\lambda+{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda)\right)\Upsilon_{n}(\lambda)\\
&\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{n}(\lambda)\right|\leq C_{\#}\left(\lambda^{2}\sigma_{n}^{-3}L_{n}^{2}n+n\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\right),\end{split}

concluding the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

We may assume that
n\geq\max\{2L_{n},n_{1}\}
, where
n_{1}
is from Lemma
4.2
since otherwise the lemma
is trivial using (
4.3
).

For such
n
, we write, using (O-
3
)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho=&{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,L_{n},\lambda}{\mathcal{L}}_{L_{n}-1,0,\lambda}\rho\\
&={\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,L_{n},\lambda}\left(\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}\ell_{L_{n},0,\lambda}(\rho)h_{L_{n},0,\lambda}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}e^{-cL_{n}})\right)\,.\end{split}

Integrating and estimating as in (
4.6
) for each
\varpi>0
we can choose
C_{L}
such that

\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}\ell_{L_{n},0,\lambda}(\rho)=\int{\mathcal{L}}_{L_{n},0,\lambda}\rho+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}e^{-cL_{n}})=1+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}(\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}L_{n}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi})).

Putting these estimates together, we have

\begin{split}\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho&=\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,L_{n},\lambda}\left(h_{L_{n},0,\lambda}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}(\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}L_{n}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}))\right)\\
&=\alpha_{n,L_{n}+1,\lambda}\left(\ell_{n,L_{n},\lambda}(h_{L_{n},0,\lambda})+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}(\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}L_{n}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi})+e^{-c|n-L_{n}|})\right)\end{split}

Next, applying first (O-
2
) and Lemma
4.4
(a) and then Lemma
4.2
yields,

\begin{split}\left|\int_{M_{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1,0,\lambda}\rho\right|&\geq K^{-1}\left|\alpha_{n,L_{n}+1,\lambda}\alpha_{L_{n},0,\lambda}\left(\ell_{n,L_{n},\lambda}(h_{L_{n},0,\lambda})+\mathcal{O}\big(\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}L_{n}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\big)\right)\right|\\
&=K^{-1}|\alpha_{n,0,\lambda}|\frac{\ell_{n,L_{n},\lambda}(h_{L_{n},0,\lambda})+\mathcal{O}\big(\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}L_{n}+\sigma_{n}^{-\varpi}\big)}{\ell_{n,L_{n},\lambda}(h_{L_{n},0,\lambda})+\mathcal{O}\big(e^{-c\min\{L_{n},n-L_{n}\}}\big)}\\
&\geq c_{\#}|\alpha_{n,0,\lambda}|\,,\end{split}

for
\varpi
large enough and
\lambda
sufficiently small, where in the last line we have used the lower bound on

|\ell_{n,L_{n},\lambda}(h_{L_{n},0,\lambda})|
from (O-
3
).
∎

5.
Cones

In this section, we describe the machinery of complex cones necessary to verify the contraction
required by (O-
3
), and which can also imply (O-
2
). For the reader’s convenience,
we provide a self contained theory of complex cones more than sufficient for our needs.
Since we strive for simplicity, our results are not optimal; see
[
35
,
16
]
for a more complete, general, but, we believe, less readable exposition.
We will present the theory in a general context, with some tools to aid in the application of
this method to the examples: expanding maps in Section
6
and billiards in Section
7
.
We start by recalling few needed facts about real cones.

5.1.
Real Cones

Let
V
be a topological real vector space and
{\mathcal{S}}\in V^{\prime}
such that
\ell(x)=0
for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}
implies
x=0
. Define

(5.1)

C_{\mathbb{R}}=\{h\in V\setminus\{0\}\;:\;\ell(h)\geq 0,\forall\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}\}.

Note that

(5.2)

C_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cap-C_{{\mathbb{R}}}=\emptyset,

since if
h\in C_{{\mathbb{R}}}\cap-C_{{\mathbb{R}}}
, then
\ell(h)=0
for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}
; hence
h=0
, contrary to definition (
5.1
).

Moreover, assume that there exists
\mathbbm{e}\in C_{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(5.3)

for all
h\in V
there exists
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
\lambda\mathbbm{e}-h\in C_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Then one can easily check that

(5.4)

\|h\|:=\inf\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\;:\;\ell(\lambda\mathbbm{e}\pm h)\geq 0,\;\forall\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}\}

is a norm.
9
9

9

Alternatively, it is equivalent to define this norm by
\|h\|=\inf\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\;:\;-\lambda\mathbbm{e}\preceq h\preceq\lambda\mathbbm{e}\}
, where
h\preceq g
iff
g-h\in C_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Note that
\|\mathbbm{e}\|=1
.
Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the completion of
V
in such a norm.
10
10

10

Note that the expression (
5.4
) defines the norm also on
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Since, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\ell(\|h\|\mathbbm{e}\pm h)\geq 0
, it follows that
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
.

Finally, let
{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
be the weak-
*
closure of the convex hull of
\{\lambda\ell\;:\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}\}
.
To simplify matters further, we restrict to the special case in which there exists
\mathbbm{m}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
and
\kappa\in(0,1)
such that

(5.5)

\mathbbm{m}(\mathbbm{e})=1

and, for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,

(5.6)

\mathbbm{m}(h)\geq\kappa\|h\|.

Having described the abstract setting, we can now proceed to explore its properties.
We start defining a larger cone
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}\supset C_{\mathbb{R}}
by

(5.7)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\;:\;\ell(h)\geq 0,\forall\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}\}.

Next, we define the dual cone as

(5.8)

{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}\;:\;\ell(h)\geq 0\quad\forall h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}\}\supset{\mathcal{S}}

Note that we have

(5.9)

{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\;:\;\ell(h)\geq 0,\forall\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}\}.

Hence, again,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap-{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\emptyset
.
11
11

11

Indeed, if
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}\cap-{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then
\ell(h)=0
for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}
, and
\|h\|=0
, by (
5.4
).

The cone
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
is associated with the Hilbert metric (see
[
7
, Equation (D.1.2)]
): for all
g,h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
,

\displaystyle\alpha(h,\,g)=

\displaystyle\sup\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\;|\;g-\lambda h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\}

\displaystyle\beta(h,\,g)=

\displaystyle\inf\{\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\;|\;\mu h-g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\}

\displaystyle d_{H,{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}}(h,\,g)=

\displaystyle\ln\left[\frac{\beta(h,\,g)}{\alpha(h,\,g)}\right].

From now on we will write simply
d_{H}
, if the cone is clear from the context.

Since by (
5.7
)
g-\lambda h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
iff
\ell(g-\lambda h)\geq 0
for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}
, taking the
limit
\lambda\uparrow\alpha
yields
\alpha=\inf_{\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}}\frac{\ell(g)}{\ell(h)}
.
Alternatively, using (
5.9
) in the same way, we have

\alpha=\inf_{\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{R}}}}\frac{\ell(g)}{\ell(h)}=\inf_{\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}}\frac{\ell(g)}{\ell(h)}
.
Arguing similarly for
\beta
yields

(5.10)

d_{H}(h,g)=\sup_{\ell,p\in{\mathcal{S}}}\ln\frac{\ell(h)p(g)}{\ell(g)p(h)}=\sup_{\ell,p\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}}\ln\frac{\ell(h)p(g)}{\ell(g)p(h)}.

Let us recall the relevance of real cones for our context.

Theorem 5.1

(
[
28
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
be real Banach spaces with real cones
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
as above. Let
L\in L({\mathcal{B}}_{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{2})
, such that
L{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
and

\Delta=\sup_{x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}d_{H,{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lx,Ly).

Then, for all
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
we have

d_{H,{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)\leq\tanh(\Delta/4)d_{H,{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g).

Lemma 5.2

(
[
28
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

Given
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}
for which
\|f\|=\|g\|
,

\|h-g\|\leq\left(e^{d_{H}(h,g)}-1\right)\|f\|.

In the next section, we describe how the above results can be extended to the case of operators acting on complex Banach spaces, see, in particular, Theorem
5.15
and Lemma
5.11
.

5.2.
Complex Cones

Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}
be the complexification of
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
as detailed in Lemma
2.5
.

Definition 5.3

(Complex cone and its dual)
.

The associated complex cone is defined as
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}:={\mathbb{C}}_{*}\cdot({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}+i{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}})
, where
{\mathbb{C}}_{*}={\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
.
We may also write
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}_{1}\cdot({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}+i{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}})
, where
{\mathbb{C}}_{1}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z|=1\}
.
We define the dual cone by

{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}=\{\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\;:\;\ell(h)\neq 0\quad\forall h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}\}.

For any element
\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
, its action on
h+ig\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
h,g\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, is naturally defined by
\ell(h+ig)=\ell(h)+i\ell(g)
.

Then, for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, letting
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
|z|=1
and
zh=x+iy
with
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
,

(5.11)

\begin{split}|\mathbbm{m}(h)|&=|\mathbbm{m}(x+iy)|=\sqrt{\mathbbm{m}(x)^{2}+\mathbbm{m}(y)^{2}}\geq\kappa\sqrt{\|x\|^{2}+\|y\|^{2}}\\
&\geq\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}\|x+iy\|=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}{\|h\|},\end{split}

where in the second line we have used (
A.2
).
In addition, if
\|u\|\leq 1
, then

\mathbbm{e}+\frac{1}{2}u\succeq\mathbbm{e}-\frac{1}{2}\|u\|\mathbbm{e}\succeq\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{e}\succeq 0

that is the interior of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, and hence of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, is not empty. That is
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a
regular cone
in the language of
[
35
, Defintion 3.2-(3)]
. Moreover, for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
such that
\|\ell\|<\kappa
we have
\mathbbm{m}+\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
, so also
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
has non empty interior.

The first statement of the following Lemma is contained in
[
35
, Proposition 5.2]
, while the second is the first part of the proof of
[
16
, Lemma 4.1]
.
12
12

12

Note however that in
[
16
, Lemma 4.1]
it is assumed that

x,y
are linearly independent, which is not necessary.

Lemma 5.4
.

We have the following characterizations.

a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}=\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}\;:\;\forall\ell,m\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\Re\left(\ell(h)\overline{m(h)}\right)\geq 0\}
.

b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}=\{\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}\;:\;\forall x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})>0\}
.

c)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\supset\{\pm\ell\pm ip\;:\;\ell,p\in\mathring{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}\}=:\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,
where
\mathring{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\;:\;\ell(x)>0\,\forall x\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\}
.

Proof.

(a) To prove that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}\;:\;\forall\ell,m\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\Re\left(\ell(h)\overline{m(h)}\right)\geq 0\}
, check that
\Re\left(\ell(zh)\overline{m(zh)}\right)=|z|^{2}\Re\left(\ell(h)\overline{m(h)}\right)
, for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}_{*}
. Also, by defintion, for each
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
there exists
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
zh=x+iy
with
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Finally, for
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\Re\left(\ell(x+iy)\overline{m(x+iy)}\right)=\ell(x)m(x)+\ell(y)m(y)\geq 0
.

To prove the opposite inclusion, let
h\in\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}\;:\;\forall\ell,m\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\Re\left(\ell(h)\overline{m(h)}\right)\geq 0\}
. We claim that there exists
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\tilde{h}:=zh=x+iy
with
x\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Indeed, let
h=u+iv
,
u,v\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
and assume that
u,v\not\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}\cup-{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. If
u=\lambda v
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then we can choose
\ell,m\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
such that
\ell(u)>0
and
\lambda m(u)<0
, so
\Re\left(\ell(h)\overline{m(h)}\right)=\lambda\ell(u)m(u)<0
contrary to assumptions. Hence
u,v
must be linearly independent in
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Note that there must exist
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
such that

\ell(u)=0
and
\ell(v)\neq 0
.
13
13

13

Indeed, by definition
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
and if
\pm u\not\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then there must exist
\ell_{1},p\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\ell_{1}(u),p(-u)<0
. Hence,
\ell=\ell_{1}-\frac{\ell_{1}(u)}{p(u)}p\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
and
\ell(u)=0
. Next, if for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
such that
\ell(u)=0
we have also
\ell(v)=0
, then the same must happen for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
(since
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
has a non empty interior) and this is impossible by the Hahn-Banch theorem.
Thus
\Re\left(\ell(h)\overline{m(h)}\right)=\ell(v)m(v)
, and choosing
m\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
such that
\ell(v)m(v)<0
leads to a contradiction. Hence, if
v\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
we are done, if
v\in-{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
then we choose
z=-1
, if
u\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
we choose
z=i
and finally if
u\in-{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
we choose
z=-i
.

Then for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\ell(\tilde{h})\neq 0
, we can write
\ell(\tilde{h})|\ell(\tilde{h})|^{-1}=e^{i\theta_{\ell}}
,
\theta_{\ell}\in[0,\pi]
. Suppose that there exists
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\pi\geq|\theta_{\ell_{2}}-\theta_{\ell_{1}}|>\pi/2
, then

\Re(\ell_{2}(h)\overline{\ell_{1}(h)})=|z|^{-2}|\ell_{2}(\tilde{h})\ell_{1}(\tilde{h})|\cos(\theta_{\ell_{2}}-\theta_{\ell_{1}})<0

contrary to the assumption. Thus there exists
\varphi\in[0,\pi/2]
such that for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
\ell(\tilde{h})\neq 0
,
\theta_{\ell}\in[\varphi-\pi/4,\varphi+\pi/4]
. Let
e^{-i\varphi}\tilde{h}=u+iv
, then
\ell(u+iv)=|\ell(u+iv)|e^{i(\theta_{\ell}-\varphi)}
which implies
\ell(u)\geq|\ell(v)|
, that is
\ell(u\pm v)\geq 0
. This means that
u\pm v\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Accordingly, setting

x+iy=(1+i)e^{-i\varphi}\tilde{h}=(1+i)(u+iv)=u-v+i(u+v)
we have that
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
, hence
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Next, we prove statement (b).
Let
\ell\in\{\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}\;:\;\forall x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})>0\}
.
If there exist
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\ell(x+iy)=0
, then
\ell(x)=-i\ell(y)
and
\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})=\Re(-i|\ell(y)|^{2})=0
contrary to the assumption.
Hence
\{\ell\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}\;:\;\forall x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}},\;\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})>0\}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
.

To prove the opposite inclusion, let
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
. Suppose that there exist
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})\leq 0
and write
\ell(x)=re^{i\theta}\neq 0
,
\ell(y)=se^{i\varphi}\neq 0
, with
\theta,\varphi\in[-\pi,\pi)
. Then
0\geq\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})=rs\cos(\theta-\varphi)
, so
\frac{3}{2}\pi\geq|\theta-\varphi|\geq\pi/2
.
Define

z=-se^{-i(\theta-\varphi)}x+ry=-s\cos(\theta-\varphi)x+ry+is\sin(\theta-\varphi)x=:u+iv

then
\ell(z)=0
. Note that
z\neq 0
since
-s\cos(\theta-\varphi)\geq 0
hence
0\neq u\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. If
\sin(\theta-\varphi)\geq 0
, then also
v\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Otherwise
iz=u_{1}+iv_{1}
with
u_{1},v_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus there exists
w\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
such that
\ell(w)=0
, contradicting the hypothesis.

Finally, we prove statement (c).
Let
\pm\ell,\pm p\in\mathring{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then for each
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\Re((\ell+ip)(x)\cdot(\ell-ip)(y))=\ell(x)\ell(y)+p(x)p(y)>0,

which implies
\ell+ip\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
by part (b) of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

For each
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
we have

\|\ell\|^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2}|\ell(\mathbbm{e})|.

Proof.

If
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
, then, when acting on
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
it can be written as
\ell(x)=\ell_{1}(x)+i\ell_{2}(x)
.
By Lemma
5.4
(b) we have, for each
x\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
,

0\leq\Re(\ell(\|x\|\mathbbm{e}-x)\overline{\ell(\|x\|\mathbbm{e}+x)})=\ell_{1}(\|x\|\mathbbm{e}-x)\ell_{1}(\|x\|\mathbbm{e}+x)+\ell_{2}(\|x\|\mathbbm{e}-x)\ell_{2}(\|x\|\mathbbm{e}+x)

which implies

\begin{split}|\ell(x)|&\leq\|x\||\ell(\mathbbm{e})|.\end{split}

If
x+iy\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, then

|\ell(x+iy)|\leq|\ell(\mathbbm{e})|(\|x\|+\|y\|)\leq\sqrt{2}|\ell(\mathbbm{e})|\|x+iy\|.

∎

Definition 5.6
.

We call the complex cone linearly convex if for each
g\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}

we can find
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\ell(g)=0
.
14
14

14

Note that this definition is a bit different from the one in
[
17
, Section 3]
. But it has the advantage of simplifying the proof of Lemma
5.8
.

Remark 5.7
.

Note that if
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is linearly convex, then

{\mathcal{C}}_{*}:=\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{C}}\;:\;\ell(h)\neq 0\;\forall\;\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}\}={\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}.

Indeed, if
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{*}
, then
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, otherwise, by linear convexity, there exists
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\ell(h)=0
, contrary to the assumption. On the other hand, if
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then for each
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}
, by Lemma
5.4
(c) and Definition
5.3
, we have that
\ell(h)\neq 0
; hence,
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{*}
.

The next Lemma corresponds to
[
17
, Lemma 4.1]
.

Lemma 5.8
.

The cone
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is linearly convex.

Proof.

Let
x+iy\not\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
,
x,y\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, then by the first statement of Lemma
5.4
, there exists
\tilde{\ell},\tilde{p}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
such that

\tilde{\ell}(x)\tilde{p}(x)+\tilde{\ell}(y)\tilde{p}(y)=\Re(\tilde{\ell}(x+iy)\tilde{p}(x-iy))<0.

Note that, setting
p=\tilde{p}+\varepsilon\mathbbm{m}
and
\ell=\tilde{\ell}+\varepsilon\mathbbm{m}
, with
\varepsilon>0
, then
\ell,p\in\mathring{{\mathcal{C}}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
and for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small we still have

(5.12)

\ell(x)p(x)+\ell(y)p(y)<0.

Define

\begin{split}&q=[\alpha_{p}\ell(x)+p(x)]\ell-i\ell(y)[\alpha_{p}\ell+p]\\
&\alpha_{p}=-\frac{p(x)\ell(x)+\ell(y)p(y)}{\ell(x)^{2}+\ell(y)^{2}}>0.\end{split}

One can check that
q(x+iy)=0
and
q\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
.
∎

In order to construct a metric on the cone, for each
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
define

(5.13)

E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)=\left\{\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(g)}\;:\;\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}\right\}\,.

For future use, it is convenient to record the following facts.

Lemma 5.9
.

For each
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
we have

E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)=\left\{\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(g)}\;:\;\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}\right\}=:\widehat{E}_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g).

Proof.

Clelarly
\widehat{E}_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)\subset E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)
.
On the other hand, note that if
\zeta\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)
, then there exists
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
such that
\ell(h-\zeta g)=0
. But this implies
h-\zeta g\not\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, otherwise, by definition, there would exist
z
such that
z(h-\zeta g)=x+iy
with
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
and this would imply
\Re(\ell(x)\overline{\ell(y)})=0
contrary to Lemma
5.4
(b). Accordingly, by Lemma
5.8
there exists

\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
such that
\ell(h-\zeta g)=0
