, hence
\zeta\in\widehat{E}_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)
. That is
E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)=\widehat{E}_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)
and the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

For each
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,
z\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g)
iff
zg-h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

If
z\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g)
, then, by Lemma
5.9
, we have that for all
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}

z\neq\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(g)}
, that is
\ell(h-zg)\neq 0
. Hence, by Remark
5.7
,
zg-h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
. On the other hand, if
zg-h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then again by by Remark
5.7
, we have that, for each
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,
\ell(zg-h)\neq 0
. Hence,
z\neq\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(g)}
, that is
z\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g)
.
∎

Next, we define the key object in the theory,

(5.14)

\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)=\ln\frac{\sup_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)}|z|}{\inf_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)}|z|}=\sup_{z,w\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)}\ln\left|\frac{z}{w}\right|\,.

As a first result, we have a version of
[
16
, Lemma 2.6]
showing the relevance of
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, such that
\mathbbm{m}(h)=\mathbbm{m}(g)
,
|\mathbbm{m}(g)|=1
.
Then

\|h-g\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g).

Proof.

There exists
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
|z|=1
such that
\mathbbm{m}(zh)=\mathbbm{m}(zg)=1
. Let
\tilde{h}=zh
and
\tilde{g}=zg
. Also, by definition
\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g)=\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\tilde{h},\tilde{g})
. Then, since
\mathbbm{m}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
,

a:=\inf_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{h},\tilde{g})}|z|\leq\left|\frac{\mathbbm{m}(\tilde{h})}{\mathbbm{m}(\tilde{g})}\right|=1\leq\sup_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{h},\tilde{g})}|z|=:b

Note that if
a=0
or
b=\infty
, then the Lemma is trivially satisfied. For each
0<\alpha<a\leq 1\leq b<\beta
we have
\alpha\tilde{g}-\tilde{h},\beta\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
. Indeed, if
\alpha\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\not\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
, then, since the cone is linearly convex (see Definition
5.6
), there would exist
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
such that
\ell(\alpha\tilde{g}-\tilde{h})=0
, but then
\alpha=\frac{\ell(\tilde{h})}{\ell(\tilde{g})}\geq a
contrary to the assumption; the argument for
\beta\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the same.

We can then write
(\beta-\alpha)(\tilde{g}-\tilde{h})=(\beta-1)(\alpha\tilde{g}-\tilde{h})+(1-\alpha)(\beta\tilde{g}-\tilde{h})
, and, recalling (
5.11
), we have

\begin{split}\|\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\|&\leq\frac{\beta-1}{\beta-\alpha}\|\alpha\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\|+\frac{1-\alpha}{\beta-\alpha}\|\beta\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\|\\
&\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\left[\frac{\beta-1}{\beta-\alpha}|\mathbbm{m}(\alpha\tilde{g}-\tilde{h})|+\frac{1-\alpha}{\beta-\alpha}|\mathbbm{m}(\beta\tilde{g}-\tilde{h})|\right]\\
&\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\frac{(\beta-1)(1-\alpha)}{\beta-\alpha}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\frac{(\sqrt{\beta}-\sqrt{\alpha})^{2}-(1-\sqrt{\alpha\beta})^{2}}{\beta-\alpha}\\
&\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\frac{\sqrt{\beta}-\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{\beta}+\sqrt{\alpha}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\ln\sqrt{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\ln\frac{\beta}{\alpha}.\end{split}

where, in the last line, we have used that, for
x\geq 1
,
\ln x\geq\frac{x-1}{x+1}
. Taking the inf on
\beta
and the sup on
\alpha
yields

\|g-h\|=\|\tilde{g}-\tilde{h}\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\tilde{h},\tilde{g})=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g).

∎

For
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
we have
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(h,zh)=0
. It is then natural to define the equivalence relation
h\sim g
iff
h=zg
for some
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
be the quotient of
\{h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}\;:\;\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(h,\mathbbm{e})<\infty\}
.
The following is similar to
[
17
, Theorem 3.1]
.
15
15

15

This result is not used in the present paper; we include it for completeness.

Theorem 5.12
.

The space
(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}},\delta_{\mathcal{C}})
is a complete metric space.

Proof.

Clearly,
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(g,h)=\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)
. For each
h,g,f\in\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
and
\ell\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
we have

\inf_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)}|z|\inf_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(g,f)}|z|\leq\left|\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(g)}\right|\,\left|\frac{\ell(g)}{\ell(f)}\right|=\left|\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(f)}\right|\leq\sup_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)}|z|\sup_{z\in E_{\mathcal{C}}(g,f)}|z|,

hence, by (
5.14
) and taking the sup and inf on
\ell
, the triangle inequality follows.

Next, if
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(h,g)=0
, then by Lemma
5.11
we have
\frac{h}{\mathbbm{m}(h)}=\frac{g}{\mathbbm{m}(g)}
, that is
h\sim g
.

As for the completeness, it follows from Lemma
5.11
that if
\{h_{n}\}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
is a Cauchy sequence with respect to
\delta_{\mathcal{C}}
, then

\left\|\frac{1}{\mathbbm{m}(h_{n})}h_{n}-\frac{1}{\mathbbm{m}(h_{m})}h_{m}\right\|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\kappa}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h_{n},h_{m}).

So
\frac{1}{\mathbbm{m}(h_{n})}h_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, hence it has a limit
h_{*}
, and by Lemma
5.4
(a),
h_{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
.
Note that, for each
g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
q\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
with
|q(\mathbbm{e})|=1
, recalling equation (
5.11
),
16
16

16

Note that, since
\mathbbm{m}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{*}
,
\left|\frac{\mathbbm{m}(h)}{\mathbbm{m}(g)}\right|\in\overline{E_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,g)}
.

e^{\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{e},g)}\geq\left|\frac{\mathbbm{m}(g)q(\mathbbm{e})}{\mathbbm{m}(\mathbbm{e})q(g)}\right|\geq c_{\#}\frac{\|g\|}{|q(g)|}\,.

Hence,
|q(h_{*})|\geq c_{\#}e^{-\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{e},h_{*})}\|h_{*}\|\geq c_{\#}
, since
\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{e},h_{*})\leq\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{e},h_{m})+1
, for
m
large enough.
For all
\ell,p\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
let
\tilde{\ell}=\ell(\mathbbm{e})^{-1}\ell
and
\tilde{p}=p(\mathbbm{e})^{-1}p
, then,
setting
\tilde{h}_{n}=h_{n}/\mathbbm{m}(h_{n})
and recalling Lemma
5.5
,

\left|\frac{\ell(h_{*})p(h_{n})}{\ell(h_{n})p(h_{*})}\right|=\left|\frac{\tilde{\ell}(h_{*})\tilde{p}(\tilde{h}_{n})}{\tilde{\ell}(\tilde{h}_{n})\tilde{p}(h_{*})}\right|\leq\frac{1+\frac{|\tilde{p}(h_{*}-\tilde{h}_{n})|}{\tilde{p}(h_{*})}}{1-\frac{|\tilde{\ell}(h_{*}-\tilde{h}_{n})|}{\tilde{\ell}(h_{*})}}\leq\frac{1+C_{\#}\|h_{*}-\tilde{h}_{n}\|}{1-C_{\#}\|h_{*}-\tilde{h}_{n}\|}

which, taking the sup on
\ell,p
implies
\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(h_{n},h_{*})=0
.
∎

The previous result is nontrivial provided
\{h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}:\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,\mathbbm{e})<\infty\}\neq\emptyset
. This
follows from the fact that
\mathbbm{e}
is in the interior of
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, which is verified by the following lemma.

Lemma 5.13
.

For any
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
and for all
z\in\mathbb{{\mathbb{C}}}
with
|z|<(\sqrt{2}\|h\|)^{-1}
we have

\mathbbm{e}+zh\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
and
\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{e},\mathbbm{e}+zh)\leq\ln\left(\frac{1+|z|\sqrt{2}\|h\|}{1-|z|\sqrt{2}\|h\|}\right)<\infty
.

Proof.

We begin by claiming that if
h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, then
h+z\mathbbm{e}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
|z|>\sqrt{2}\|h\|
.
Indeed, by Remark
5.7
and Lemma
5.8
, if
h+z\mathbbm{e}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
, then
there exists
\ell\in\hat{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
such that
\ell(h+z\mathbbm{e})=0
. But then applying Lemma
5.5
, one has

|z\ell(\mathbbm{e})|=|\ell(h)|\leq\sqrt{2}\|h\||\ell(\mathbbm{e})|,

which is a contradiction.

With the claim proved, it follows that
\mathbbm{e}+zh\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
for all
|z|<(\sqrt{2}\|h\|)^{-1}
. Moreover, for any

\ell\in\hat{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\prime}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,

\frac{\ell(\mathbbm{e}+zh)}{\ell(\mathbbm{e})}=1+z\frac{\ell(h)}{\ell(\mathbbm{e})}\,,

so that according to the definition (
5.14
) and using again Lemma
5.5
,

\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathbbm{e}+zh,\mathbbm{e})\leq\ln\left(\frac{1+|z|\sqrt{2}\|h\|}{1-|z|\sqrt{2}\|h\|}\right)\,,

as required.
∎

The metric
\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}
also provides a partial converse to Lemma
5.5
, as follows.

Lemma 5.14
.

For any
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
and all
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
,

|\ell(h)|\geq e^{-\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,\mathbbm{e})}\tfrac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}\|h\||\ell(\mathbbm{e})|\,.

Proof.

Let
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
and suppose
D=\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}}(h,\mathbbm{e})
. Then by definition (
5.14
),
for any
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
,

e^{D}\geq\frac{|\ell(\mathbbm{e})\mathbbm{m}(h)|}{|\ell(h)\mathbbm{m}(\mathbbm{e})|}\geq\frac{|\ell(\mathbbm{e})|}{|\ell(h)|}\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{2}}\|h\|\,,

where we have used (
5.11
). The lemma follows.
∎

The next is a basic result in
[
16
]
. We provide a detailed proof, although it is essentially as in
[
16
]
, because our definitions differ slightly from the ones in
[
16
]
.

Theorem 5.15

(
[
17
, Theorem 3.1 (iii)]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
be complex Banach spaces with complex cones
{\mathcal{C}}_{1},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, satisfying hyotheses (
5.1
), (
5.3
) and (
5.6
). Let
L\in L({\mathcal{B}}_{1},{\mathcal{B}}_{2})
, such that
L{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
and

\Delta=\sup_{x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lx,Ly)
. Then, for all
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
we have

\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)\leq\tanh(\Delta/4)\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g).

Proof.

Let
g,h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
. If
g=zh
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, then we have seen that
\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)=\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)=0
, hence the theorem is trivially true. We can then assume
g,h
are
linearly independent. Let
\mu,\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
be such that

(5.15)

\begin{split}&|\mu|=M:=\sup\{|z|\;:\;z\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)\}\\
&|\lambda|=m:=\inf\{|z|\;:\;z\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)\}.\end{split}

Since
if
M=\infty
or
m=0
, the statement is trivial, we can assume
\infty>M>m>0
.
Indeed it must be that
M>m
, otherwise
M=m
and
E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)\subset\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\;:\;|z|=m\}
. But Lemma
5.4
(a) implies that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathbb{C}}\cup\{0\}
is closed, hence its complement is open, and so is
E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)
by Lemma
5.10
,
contradicting the hypothesis that it is contained in a circle.
The openness of
E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)
implies that

\lambda,\mu\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)
. Hence, by Lemma
5.10
,

\mu g-h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\,,\quad h-\lambda g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}.

Accordingly, by assumption,

L(\mu g-h)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\,,\quad L(h-\lambda g)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

and,

(5.16)

\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(L(\mu g-h),L(h-\lambda g))\leq\Delta.

Let
z\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(L(h-\lambda g),L(\mu g-h))
. By Lemma
5.10
this is equivalent to

zL(\mu g-h)-L(h-\lambda g)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}

or

\frac{z\mu+\lambda}{z+1}Lg-Lh\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}.

Accordingly, if we define the Möbious transformation
\psi(z)=\frac{z\mu+\lambda}{z+1}
, then

\psi(E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(L(h-\lambda g),L(\mu g-h)))=E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg).

Recalling (
5.16
) and (
5.14
)

(5.17)

e^{\Delta}\geq\sup_{z,w\in\psi^{-1}(E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg))}\left|\frac{z}{w}\right|=\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)}\left|\frac{\mu-\alpha}{\mu-\beta}\right|\,\left|\frac{\lambda-\beta}{\lambda-\alpha}\right|\,.

Note that, by Lemma
5.10
and the cone invariance, if
z\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)
, then
zLg-Lh\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, hence
z\not\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)
. It follows that
E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)\subset E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)
,
hence in equation (
5.17
) we have
m<|\alpha|,|\beta|<M
.

Next, we choose
\lambda,\mu
to maximize the right-hand side of the above expression. Note that
\phi(z)=\frac{z-\beta}{z-\alpha}
is a Möbius transformation, hence it maps the circle
|z|=r
into the circle of center

c(r)=\frac{\beta\overline{\alpha}-r^{2}}{|\alpha|^{2}-r^{2}}
and radius
R(r)=\frac{r|\beta-\alpha|}{|\,|\alpha|^{2}-r^{2}\,|}
.
17
17

17

Indeed, the reader can easily check that
|\phi^{-1}(x+iy)|=r
implies

x^{2}+y^{2}-2\frac{\Re(\beta\overline{\alpha}-r^{2})x+\Im(\beta\overline{\alpha}-r^{2})y}{|\alpha|^{2}-r^{2}}+\frac{|\beta|^{2}-r^{2}}{|\alpha|^{2}-r^{2}}=0.

As observed above, the entire circles of radius
m
and
M
lie in the
complement of
E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)
.
Thus,

\begin{split}\sup_{|\lambda|=m}\left|\frac{\lambda-\beta}{\lambda-\alpha}\right|&=|c(m)|+R(m)=\frac{|\beta\overline{\alpha}-m^{2}|+m|\alpha-\beta|}{|\alpha|^{2}-m^{2}}\\
&\geq\frac{|\beta||\alpha|-m^{2}+m|\beta|-m|\alpha|}{|\alpha|^{2}-m^{2}}=\frac{|\beta|-m}{|\alpha|-m}.\end{split}

Reversing the roles of
\alpha
and
\beta
yields the analogous estimate on
C(M)
,

\sup_{|\mu|=M}\left|\frac{\mu-\alpha}{\mu-\beta}\right|=\frac{|M^{2}-\alpha\overline{\beta}|+M|\beta-\alpha|}{M^{2}-|\beta|^{2}}\geq\frac{M-|\alpha|}{M-|\beta|}.

Substituting in (
5.17
) yields

e^{\Delta}\geq\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in E(Lh,Lg)}\frac{(M-|\alpha|)(|\frac{\beta}{\alpha}||\alpha|-m)}{(M-|\frac{\beta}{\alpha}||\alpha|)(|\alpha|-m)}.

Remark that the above supremum occurs when
|\alpha|<|\beta|
, which we will assume going forward. With this condition, the ratio

\frac{(M-|\alpha|)(|\beta|-m)}{(M-|\beta|)(|\alpha|-m)}\geq 1
.
We can then study the function
\phi(t)=\frac{(M-t)(|\frac{\beta}{\alpha}|t-m)}{(M-|\frac{\beta}{\alpha}|t)(t-m)}
for
m<t<M\frac{|\alpha|}{|\beta|}
.
This function is positive, has vertical asymptotes at the endpoints of its domain
18
18

18

Note that

t=|\alpha|
is indeed in this domain since, recalling also (
5.15
),
m<|\alpha|
by definition and
M|\alpha|/|\beta|>|\alpha|
since
M>|\beta|
.
and has a minimum at
t_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{mM|\alpha|}{|\beta|}}\in(m,M\frac{|\alpha|}{|\beta|})
.
Hence,

e^{\Delta}\geq\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)}\phi(t_{0})=\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)}\frac{\left(\sqrt{\frac{M|\beta|}{m|\alpha|}}-1\right)^{2}}{\left(\sqrt{\frac{M}{m}}-\sqrt{\frac{|\beta|}{|\alpha|}}\right)^{2}}.

Since the above right-hand side is increasing with respect to
\frac{|\beta|}{|\alpha|}
for
0<\frac{|\beta|}{|\alpha|}<\frac{M}{m}
, and, by definition,
\sup_{\alpha,\beta\in E_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}(Lh,Lg)}\frac{|\beta|}{|\alpha|}=e^{\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}(Lg,Lh)}}=:e^{\theta_{2}}
, we have, setting

e^{\theta_{1}}:=\frac{M}{m}=e^{\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}(h,g)}
, and recalling that by construction,
\theta_{1}>\theta_{2}
,

\begin{split}e^{\frac{\Delta}{2}}&\geq\frac{e^{\frac{\theta_{2}+\theta_{1}}{2}}-1}{e^{\frac{\theta_{1}}{2}}-e^{\frac{\theta_{2}}{2}}}=\frac{e^{\frac{\theta_{2}+\theta_{1}}{4}}-e^{-\frac{\theta_{2}+\theta_{1}}{4}}}{e^{\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{4}}-e^{-\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{4}}}=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}+\theta_{1}}{4}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{4}\right)}\\
&=\frac{\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)+\cosh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)}{\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)-\cosh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)\sinh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)}=\frac{\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)+\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)}{\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)-\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)}.\end{split}

The above implies

\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{2}}{4}\right)\leq\frac{e^{\frac{\Delta}{2}}-1}{1+e^{\frac{\Delta}{2}}}\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)=\tanh\left(\frac{\Delta}{4}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}}{4}\right)\leq\tanh\left(\frac{\theta_{1}\tanh\left(\frac{\Delta}{4}\right)}{4}\right)

since, for
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
x\geq 0
,
\tanh(\gamma x)\geq\gamma\tanh(x)
. Because the hyperbolic tangent is increasing, we finally have
\theta_{2}\leq\tanh\left(\frac{\Delta}{4}\right)\theta_{1}
,
and the theorem follows.
∎

Next, we provide a simplified but less optimal version of
[
17
, Theorem 4.5]
, which suffices for our needs.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,{\mathbb{C}}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
,
\mathbbm{e}_{i}
and
\mathbbm{m}_{i}
be as in the introduction of the appendix.
We assume (
5.4
) and (
5.6
) hold for both
i=1
and
i=2
.
We start by relating the real diameter with the complex one.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
A\in L({\mathcal{B}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}},{\mathcal{B}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}})
such that
A({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}
with

\operatorname{diam}_{d_{H}}(A({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}))=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}<\infty

then
19
19

19

A more sophisticated argument would yield a better constant, independent on
\kappa
, see
[
17
]
, but this estimate is more than sufficient for our needs and in the spirit to present the argument in its simplest form.

\Delta_{{\mathbb{C}}}:=\operatorname{diam}_{\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}}(A({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}))\leq 8\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}+2\ln[3\sqrt{2}\kappa^{-2}].

Proof.

By (
5.10
) we have, for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}

e^{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\geq\frac{\ell(Ah)\mathbbm{m}_{2}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})}{\mathbbm{m}_{2}(Ah)\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})}\geq e^{-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}

which, recalling (
5.6
) and the normalization
\mathbbm{m}_{i}(\mathbbm{e}_{i})=1
, implies

(5.18)

\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})\|Ah\|e^{\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}\geq\ell(Ah)\geq\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})\kappa\|Ah\|e^{-\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}.

Accordingly, for all
h=z(x+iy)
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
,
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
, and
\ell,p\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}

\Re(\ell(Ah)\overline{p(Ah)})=|z|^{2}\left[\ell(Ax)p(Ax)+\ell(Ay)p(Ay)\right]\geq 0.

It follows from Lemma
5.4
(a) that
A({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}
, and, for all
\tilde{\ell},\tilde{p}\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}

and all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
, setting
\ell=|\tilde{\ell}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})|^{-1}\tilde{\ell}=\ell_{1}+i\ell_{2}
and
p=|\tilde{p}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})|^{-1}\tilde{p}=p_{1}+ip_{2}

(5.19)

\left|\frac{z\tilde{\ell}(A(x+iy))\tilde{p}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})}{\tilde{\ell}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})z\tilde{p}(A(x+iy))}\right|^{2}\leq\frac{\left[\ell_{1}(Ax)-\ell_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[\ell_{2}(Ax)+\ell_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}}{\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}}.

We use (
5.18
) to bound the numerator,

\begin{split}\left[\ell_{1}(Ax)-\ell_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[\ell_{2}(Ax)+\ell_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}&\leq 2\big(\ell_{1}(Ax)^{2}+\ell_{2}(Ay)^{2}\big)+2\big(\ell_{2}(Ax)^{2}+\ell_{1}(Ay)^{2}\big)\\
&\leq 2e^{2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\big(\|Ax\|^{2}+\|Ay\|^{2}\big)\,,\end{split}

where we have used the normalization
\ell_{1}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}+\ell_{2}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}=1
.

To derive a lower bound on the denominator in (
5.19
), we argue by cases.
Fix
b\geq 2
and
c>0
,
c<\frac{e^{-2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}\kappa}{\sqrt{e^{-4\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}\kappa^{2}+b^{2}}}<1/\sqrt{5}
.

Case 1:
p_{1}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2},p_{2}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}\geq c^{2}
.

Note that, by definition, there exists
\sigma_{i}\in\{-1,+1\}
such that
\sigma_{i}p_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
.
If
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=1
, then, recalling (
5.18
),

\begin{split}&\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}\geq\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}\\
&\geq p_{2}(Ax)^{2}+p_{1}(Ay)^{2}\geq\kappa^{2}c^{2}(\|Ax\|^{2}+\|Ay\|^{2})e^{-2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}.\end{split}

while, If
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}=-1
, then similarly,

\begin{split}&\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}\geq\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}\\
&\geq p_{1}(Ax)^{2}+p_{2}(Ay)^{2}\geq\kappa^{2}c^{2}(\|Ax\|^{2}+\|Ay\|^{2})e^{-2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}.\end{split}

Case 2: Either
p_{1}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}<c^{2}
or
p_{2}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}<c^{2}
.

We shall assume
p_{1}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}<c^{2}
, the argument for the other alternative being analogous.
In this case then,
p_{2}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})^{2}>1-c^{2}
due to the normalization of
p
.

We further refine into subcases.

Subcase A:
\|Ay\|b^{-1}\leq\|Ax\|\leq b\|Ay\|
.

Then by choice of
c
and using (
5.18
),

\begin{split}\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}&\geq\left[\sqrt{1-c^{2}}\kappa e^{-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\|Ay\|-ce^{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}b\|Ay\|\right]^{2}\\
&=\|Ay\|^{2}\left(\sqrt{1-c^{2}}\kappa e^{-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}-bce^{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\right)^{2}\,.\end{split}

Adding the analogous estimate for the second term yields the lower bound,

\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}\geq\left(\|Ax\|^{2}+\|Ay\|^{2}\right)\left(\sqrt{1-c^{2}}\kappa e^{-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}-bce^{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\right)^{2}\,.

Subcase B: Either
\|Ay\|<b^{-1}\|A_{x}\|
or
\|Ax\|<b^{-1}\|Ay\|
.

Again, it is sufficient to argue only one of the alternatives. Suppose that
\|Ay\|<b^{-1}\|Ax\|
.
Then,

\begin{split}&\left[p_{1}(Ax)-p_{2}(Ay)\right]^{2}+\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}\geq\left[p_{2}(Ax)+p_{1}(Ay)\right]^{2}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\|Ax\|^{2}\left(\sqrt{1-c^{2}}\kappa e^{-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}-b^{-1}ce^{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\right)^{2}\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\geq\frac{b^{2}}{b^{2}+1}\left(\|Ax\|^{2}+\|Ay\|^{2}\right)\left(\sqrt{1-c^{2}}\kappa e^{-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}-b^{-1}ce^{\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\right)^{2}.\end{split}

We proceed to optimize our choice of
b
and
c
to obtain a common lower bound from Cases
1 and 2. Let us choose
b=\sqrt{2}
. Then set

c=\frac{\kappa e^{-2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}}{\sqrt{\kappa^{2}e^{-4\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}+4b^{2}}}\implies\sqrt{c^{-2}-1}=2b\kappa^{-1}e^{2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}\,.

This implies that the lower bound in Subcase A is at least,

(5.20)

\left(\|Ax\|^{2}+\|Ay\|^{2}\right)\frac{\kappa^{2}c^{2}}{4}\left(c^{-2}-1\right)e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\,.

Moreover, the lower bound in Subcase B is at least as large since with
b=\sqrt{2}
,

\frac{b}{\sqrt{b^{2}+1}}\left(\sqrt{c^{-2}-1}-\kappa^{-1}b^{-1}e^{2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\right)\geq\sqrt{c^{-2}-1}\left(1-\frac{1}{2b^{2}}\right)\frac{b}{\sqrt{b^{2}+1}}>\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{c^{-2}-1}.

It remains to compare the lower bounds from Subcase A and Case 1. Due to (
5.20
),
the lower bound from Case 1 is smaller if

c^{2}<c^{2}\frac{1}{4}(c^{-2}-1)\implies c<\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\,,

which is true given our choice of
c
whenever
b\geq 1
.

Finally, putting together this common lower bound with our upper bound for the numerator in
(
5.19
), yields,

(5.21)

\left|\frac{z\tilde{\ell}(A(x+iy))\tilde{p}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})}{\tilde{\ell}(\mathbbm{e}_{2})z\tilde{p}(A(x+iy))}\right|\leq\sqrt{2}\kappa^{-1}c^{-1}e^{2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}\leq 3\sqrt{2}\kappa^{-2}e^{4\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\,.

Since

(5.22)

\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}(A(h),\mathbbm{e}_{2})\leq\operatorname{diam}_{\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}}(A({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}))\leq 2\sup_{h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}(Ah,\mathbbm{e}_{2}),

we have the lemma.
∎

We finally provide the key result that is needed in the applications to verify the hypothesis on the finiteness of the complex diameter in Theorem
5.15
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,{\mathbb{R}}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,{\mathbb{C}}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
,
\mathbbm{e}_{i}
and
\mathbbm{m}_{i}
be as in the introduction to this section (in particular, the
\mathbbm{e}_{i}
and
\mathbbm{m}_{i}
satisfy equations (
5.3
) and (
5.6
), respectively).
Let
{\mathcal{L}}\in L({\mathcal{B}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}},{\mathcal{B}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}})
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathbb{C}}\in L({\mathcal{B}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}},{\mathcal{B}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}})
.
Assume that
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\operatorname{diam}_{H}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}})):=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}<\infty
.
If there exists
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{\kappa^{2}}{12\sqrt{2}}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}})
, such that
for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
and all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
,

(5.23)

\left|\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)-\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\right|\leq\varepsilon\ell({\mathcal{L}}h).

Then
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}
, and we have

\operatorname{diam}_{\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}))\leq 8\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}+2\ln[3\sqrt{2}\kappa^{-2}]+\tfrac{\sqrt{2}}{3}\kappa^{2}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}.

Proof.

For all
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
we have using (
5.23
),

(5.24)

\begin{split}\left|\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(x+iy))-\ell({\mathcal{L}}(x+iy))\right|\leq&\left|\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}x)-\ell({\mathcal{L}}x)\right|+\left|\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}y)-\ell({\mathcal{L}}y)\right|\\
\leq&\varepsilon[\ell({\mathcal{L}}x)+\ell({\mathcal{L}}y)]\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{2}|\ell({\mathcal{L}}(x+iy))|.\end{split}

It follows that for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}
and
\ell,p\in\mathring{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
, provided
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
,

\begin{split}\Re(\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)})&=\Re\Bigg(\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}h)}+\ell(({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}-{\mathcal{L}})h)\overline{p(({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}-{\mathcal{L}})h)}\\
&+\ell(({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}-{\mathcal{L}})h)\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}h)}+\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\overline{p(({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}-{\mathcal{L}})h)}\Bigg)\\
&\geq\Re\left(\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}h)}\right)-3\sqrt{2}\varepsilon|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)||{p({\mathcal{L}}h)}|\\
&\geq\Re\left(\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}h)}\right)-6\sqrt{2}\varepsilon\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})p(\mathbbm{e}_{2})\|{\mathcal{L}}h\|\|{\mathcal{L}}h\|\end{split}

where, in the first inequality we have used (
5.24
) and in the second inequality we have
used Lemma
5.4
(c) and Lemma
5.5
.
Since we can assume
h=x+iy
with
x,y\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
, recalling (
5.18
) we have

\begin{split}\Re\left(\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}h)}\right)&=\ell({\mathcal{L}}(x))p({\mathcal{L}}x)+\ell({\mathcal{L}}y)p({\mathcal{L}}y)\\
&\geq\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})p(\mathbbm{e}_{2})\kappa^{2}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\left(\|{\mathcal{L}}x\|^{2}+\|{\mathcal{L}}y\|^{2}\right)\\
&\geq 2^{-1}\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})p(\mathbbm{e}_{2})\kappa^{2}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\|{\mathcal{L}}h\|^{2}\,,\end{split}

where we have used (
A.2
). Hence,
\Re(\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(h))\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(h))})>0
, provided
\varepsilon<\frac{\kappa^{2}}{12\sqrt{2}}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}
.

Note that if
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
, then
\ell+\alpha\mathbbm{m}\in\mathring{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
for each
\alpha>0
. Thus the above implies
\Re(\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(h))\overline{p({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(h))})\geq 0
for
all
\ell,p\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
.
Applying Lemma
5.4
(a),
we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}({\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}
.

Finally, for all
\ell,p\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
and
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{C}}}
, it follows by (
5.24
) that,

\begin{split}\left|\frac{\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)p({\mathcal{L}}h)}{\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)p({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)}\right|&\leq\frac{|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)p({\mathcal{L}}h)|+|\ell([{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}-{\mathcal{L}}]h)p({\mathcal{L}}h)|}{|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)p({\mathcal{L}}h)|-|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)p([{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}-{\mathcal{L}}]h)|}\\
&\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}{1-\sqrt{2}\varepsilon}\leq 1+4\varepsilon,\end{split}

since we assumed
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{12\sqrt{2}}
.

The above implies

(5.25)

\begin{split}\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h,\mathbbm{e}_{2})&=\sup_{\ell,p}\ln\frac{|\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)p(\mathbbm{e}_{2})|}{|\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})p({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)|}\\
&\leq\sup_{\ell,p}\ln\frac{|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)p(\mathbbm{e}_{2})|}{|\ell(\mathbbm{e}_{2})p({\mathcal{L}}h)|}+\sup_{\ell,p}\ln\frac{|\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)p({\mathcal{L}}h)|}{|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)p({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)|}\\
&\leq\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{C}}}}({\mathcal{L}}h,\mathbbm{e}_{2})+4\varepsilon\leq 4\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}+\ln[3\sqrt{2}\kappa^{-2}]+4\varepsilon\\
&\leq 4\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}+\ln[3\sqrt{2}\kappa^{-2}]+\tfrac{1}{3\sqrt{2}}\kappa^{2}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}},\end{split}

where, in the next to last inequality, we have applied (
5.21
). We conclude using
(
5.22
).
∎

We conclude with a comment on (
5.23
) that may facilitate checking it.

For each
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
let
a(h)=\Re({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h),b(h)=\Im({\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h)
. By defintion
a,b
are real linear operators on
{\mathcal{B}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}h=a(h)+ib(h)
.

Lemma 5.18
.

If, for each
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1,{\mathbb{R}}}
,

\begin{split}&\varepsilon{\mathcal{L}}h\pm 2\left[{\mathcal{L}}h-a(h)\right]\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}\\
&\varepsilon{\mathcal{L}}h\pm 2b(h)\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}\end{split}

then condition (
5.23
) is satisfied.

Proof.

By hypothesis, for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2,{\mathbb{R}}}^{\prime}
we have

\begin{split}&\varepsilon\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\geq 2\left|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h-a(h))\right|\\
&\varepsilon\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\geq 2\left|\ell(b(h))\right|.\end{split}

Accordingly,

\varepsilon\ell({\mathcal{L}}h)\geq\left(|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h-a(h))|+|\ell(-ib(h))|\right)\geq|\ell({\mathcal{L}}h-{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}(h))|.

∎

5.3.
Cone Contraction Implies Loss of Memory

In this section, we show that cone contraction implies loss of memory.
This provides a tool to easily verify, in some cases, condition (O-
3
).

Consider complex Banach spaces
{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
, cones
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}={\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}},k}\subset{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
, vectors
\mathbbm{e}_{k}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{k}
as described in Sections
2
and
5.2
.
Suppose there exist functionals
\mathbbm{m}_{k}\in{\mathbb{B}}_{k}^{\prime}

satisfying (
5.6
) with

a)

\displaystyle\inf_{k\geq 0}\kappa_{k}=\bar{\kappa}>0
.

In addition,
there are operators
{\mathbb{L}}_{k}:{\mathbb{B}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{B}}_{k+1}
satisfying the following:

b)

there exists
K>0
such that, for all
k,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have

\left|\mathbbm{m}_{k}\left({\mathbb{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathbb{L}}_{j}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\right)\right|\leq K;

c)

the operators
{\mathbb{L}}_{k}
satisfy
{\mathbb{L}}_{k}({\mathcal{C}}_{k})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{k+1}
, and the
diameter in the complex projective metric of
{\mathbb{L}}_{k}({\mathcal{C}}_{k})
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{k+1}

is uniformly bounded by
\Delta<\infty
.

Define
{\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}:={\mathbb{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathbb{L}}_{j}
and

(5.26)

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}=\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\mathbbm{e}_{j})\\
&\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}=\frac{{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\mathbbm{e}_{j}}{\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\mathbbm{e}_{j})}\\
&\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)=\frac{\mathbbm{m}_{k}\left({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h\right)}{\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\mathbbm{e}_{j})}.\end{split}

The next lemma will be helpful to check condition (O-
2
).

Lemma 5.19

(Uniform Boundedness)
.

For each
h\in{\mathbb{B}}_{j}
, we have

\begin{split}&|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}|\leq K,\quad\|\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\|_{k}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}},\quad\|\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}\|_{j}^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2}\\
&\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}h\|_{k}\leq\frac{2K}{\bar{\kappa}}\|h\|_{j}.\end{split}

Proof.

By assumption
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}|\leq K
.
Since, by assumption,
\mathbbm{m}_{k}
satisfies (
5.11
), by Definition
5.3
,
\mathbbm{m}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
so that, for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,

(5.27)

\left|\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h)\right|\geq\frac{\bar{\kappa}}{\sqrt{2}}\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h\|_{k}>0.

Hence,
\|\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\|_{k}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}
. In addition,
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)\neq 0
for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
. Accordingly,

\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
and so by Lemma
5.5

\|\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}\|^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2}
. Hence, recalling (
5.11
)

\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h\|_{k}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\left|\mathbbm{m}_{k}\left({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h\right)\right|=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\left|\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}\left(h\right)\mathbbm{m}_{k}\left({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\right)\right|\leq\frac{2K}{\bar{\kappa}}\|h\|_{j}.

∎

The following lemma will be helpful to check condition (O-
3
).

Lemma 5.20

(Loss of Memory)
.

Let
\theta=\tanh(\Delta/4)
, then for each
k,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
h\in{\mathbb{B}}_{j}
,

\begin{split}\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}&\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\|_{j}\leq\frac{2(1+\sqrt{2})}{\bar{\kappa}\theta^{2}}\Delta\theta^{k-j}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}|\,\|h\|_{j}.\end{split}

Proof.

Applying Lemma
5.11
and Theorem
5.15
, we have for all
h,g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
,

\begin{split}&\left\|\frac{{\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}h}{\mathbbm{m}_{k+1}({\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}h)}-\frac{{\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}g}{\mathbbm{m}_{k+1}({\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}g)}\right\|_{k+1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\delta_{{\mathbb{C}},k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}h,{\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}g)\\
&\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}(\tanh(\Delta/4))^{k-j-1}\delta_{{\mathbb{C}},j+1}({\mathbb{L}}_{j}h,{\mathbb{L}}_{j}g)\\
&=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\theta^{k-j-1}\delta_{{\mathbb{C}},j+1}({\mathbb{L}}_{j}h,{\mathbb{L}}_{j}g)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\theta^{k-j-1}\Delta.\end{split}

By definition
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\mathbbm{e}_{j})=1
and
\mathbbm{m}_{k}(\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j})=1
.
Moreover,
by Lemma
5.19
and Lemma
5.5
,

|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}|\leq K
,
\|\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\|_{k}\leq\sqrt{2}/\bar{\kappa}

and

\|\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}\|_{j}^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2}
.
Hence, recalling (
5.26
), for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
we have

(5.28)

\begin{split}\left\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\right\|_{k}&=\left\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h-\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h)\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\right\|_{k}\\
&\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\theta^{2}\bar{\kappa}}\theta^{k-j}\Delta\left|\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h)\right|\\
&\leq\frac{2}{\theta^{2}\bar{\kappa}}\theta^{k-j}\Delta|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}|\|h\|_{j}.\end{split}

For
h\in{\mathbb{B}}_{j}
, define
\bar{h}:=h+\sqrt{2}\|h\|_{j}\mathbbm{e}_{j}
. As in Lemma
5.13
,

\bar{h},\|h\|_{j}\mathbbm{e}_{j}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}},j}\cup\{0\}
.
Recalling
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\|h\|_{j}\mathbbm{e}_{j})=\|h\|_{j}
, as well as

\mathbbm{m}_{k}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\bar{h})=\mathbbm{m}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\bar{h})\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j})
,
we apply (
5.28
) to obtain,

\begin{split}\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}&\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\|_{k}=\|{\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}\bar{h}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\bar{h})\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}\|_{k}\\
&\leq\|\bar{h}\|_{j}\frac{2}{\bar{\kappa}\theta^{2}}\Delta\theta^{k-j}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|_{k,j}\leq\|h\|_{j}\frac{2(1+\sqrt{2})}{\bar{\kappa}\theta^{2}}\Delta\theta^{k-j}|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}|.\end{split}

∎

5.4.
A Useful Lower Bound

In this section, we show under the assumptions of Theorem
5.17
, that the lower
bound
|\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l})|\geq K^{-1}
holds uniformly in
j,k,l
, with

\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j},\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l}
defined as in (
5.26
). In addition to properties
(a)-(c) of Section
5.3
, we assume that there exist real operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}:{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1}

satisfying

d)

{\mathcal{L}}_{k}({\mathcal{C}}_{k,{\mathbb{R}}})\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{k+1,{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\operatorname{diam}_{H}({\mathcal{L}}_{k}({\mathcal{C}}_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}))\leq\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}<\infty
for all
k\geq 1
;

e)

for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}_{k+1}
and all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{k,{\mathbb{R}}}
,

|\ell({\mathbb{L}}_{k}h)-\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{k}h)|\leq\varepsilon\ell({\mathcal{L}}_{k}h)
for some
\varepsilon\in\big(0,\frac{\bar{\kappa}^{2}}{12\sqrt{2}}e^{-2\Delta_{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
.

Thus we are in the setting of Theorem
5.17
.

In this setting, we prove the lemma,

Lemma 5.21
.

Under the assumption (a)–(e), there exists
K>0
such that
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l})\geq K^{-1}
for all
0\leq l\leq j\leq k
.

Proof.

By definition,

\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)=\frac{\mathbbm{m}({\mathbb{L}}_{k-1,j}h)}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\ell,j}}\,.

Since
{\mathbb{L}}_{k,j}{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{k,{\mathbb{C}}}
and invoking (
5.11
) and property (a) of Section
5.3
, we have

\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(h)\neq 0
for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}
. Thus
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}^{\prime}
and since

\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}
, we may
apply Lemma
5.14
to obtain,

|\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l})|\geq e^{-\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}}(\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l},\mathbbm{e}_{j})}\tfrac{\bar{\kappa}}{\sqrt{2}}\|\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l}\|_{j}\,,

where we have used
\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}(\mathbbm{e}_{j})=1
.
Recalling (
5.26
) and using Lemma
5.5
, and equation (
5.5
), we have

1=\mathbbm{m}_{j}(\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l})\leq\|\mathbbm{m}_{j}\|_{j}^{\prime}\|\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l}\|_{j}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l}\|_{j}.

It remains to show that
\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}}(\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l},\mathbbm{e}_{j})\leq C
, where
C<\infty
is independent of
j
and
l
.
This follows from the cone contraction provided for each
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
. In particular, remark that

{\mathbb{L}}_{j-1,l}{\mathcal{C}}_{l,{\mathbb{C}}}\subset{\mathbb{L}}_{j-1}{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1,{\mathbb{C}}}
, so that

\boldsymbol{h}_{j,l}\in{\mathbb{L}}_{j-1}{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1,{\mathbb{C}}}
. Thus it suffices to show that

\delta_{j,{\mathcal{C}}}(h,\mathbbm{e}_{j})\leq C
for each
h\in{\mathbb{L}}_{j-1}{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1,{\mathbb{C}}}
for some
C
independent of
j
.

To see this, apply Theorem
5.17
, and in particular (
5.25
), with

{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}={\mathbb{L}}_{j-1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}
to obtain for each
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{j-1,{\mathbb{C}}}
,

\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}}({\mathbb{L}}_{j-1}h,\mathbbm{e}_{j})\leq\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}h,\mathbbm{e}_{j})+4\varepsilon\,,

with
\varepsilon=\frac{\bar{\kappa}^{2}}{12\sqrt{2}}e^{-2\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}}
, applying properties (d) and (e).
Finally, applying Lemma
5.16
and in particular (
5.21
) with
A={\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}
yields,

\delta_{{\mathcal{C}}_{j,{\mathbb{C}}}}({\mathbb{L}}_{j-1}h,\mathbbm{e}_{j})\leq 4\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}}+\ln\big(3\sqrt{2}\bar{\kappa}^{-2}\big)+4\varepsilon\,,

completing the proof of the claim.
∎

6.
Application: Smooth Expanding Maps

6.1.
Sequential Expanding Maps

We consider the setting illustrated in Section
2.1
and prove Theorem
2.1
. To this end, we first check the conditions of Theorem
2.7
.
Theorem
2.1
will then follow trivially as explained in Section
6.5
.

6.1.1.
Verifying Conditions (C-
1
)-(C-
4
)

Without loss of generality, we can rescale the distance
d
so that the diameter of
M
is one and the Riemannian volume so that the volume of
M
is also one. We set
M_{k}=M
and
\mu_{k}=
Lebesgue. First of all, for
a>0
, we consider the cones
C_{k}={\mathcal{C}}_{a}
for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

{\mathcal{C}}_{a}=\left\{h\in C^{1}(M,{\mathbb{R}})\;:\;\|\nabla h(x)\|\leq ah(x)\,\,\forall x\in M\right\}\setminus\{0\}.

Note that (C-
1
) is trivially satisfied. Then setting
\mathbbm{e}(x)=1\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
,
if
h\in C^{1}
, then
(a^{-1}\|\nabla h\|_{\infty}+\|h\|_{\infty})\mathbbm{e}+h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
;
that is, (C-
3
) holds true.
Indeed, it follows immediately from (
2.5
) that
for
h\in C^{1}(M,{\mathbb{C}})
,

\|g\|:=\|g\|_{k}=\sup_{x\in M}\{a^{-1}\|\nabla g(x)\|+|g(x)|\},

thus
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}=C^{1}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
and

{\mathbb{B}}_{k}=C^{1}(M,{\mathbb{C}})
.

Next, suppose

g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
and
x_{0}\in M
is such that
20
20

20

Here and throughout this section, the integral
is taken with respect to the volume measure on
M
, which has been normalized to be a probability measure.

g(x_{0})=\int_{M}g
.

If
\gamma\in C^{1}([0,1],M)
is a geodesic (parametrized by arclength) that connects
x
and
x_{0}
, then

\left|\frac{d}{dt}g(\gamma(t))\right|\leq\|\nabla g(\gamma(t))\|\leq ag(\gamma(t)),

hence

(6.1)

e^{a}\int_{M}g\geq g(x)\geq e^{-a}\int_{M}g\,,\quad\mbox{for all $x\in M$}.

Defining the transfer operators
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
as in (
2.3
) with respect to the
volume measure yields,

{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h(x)=\sum_{y\in f_{k}^{-1}(x)}\frac{h(y)}{\left|\det(D_{y}f_{k})\right|}.

Hence, for some
\bar{D}\leq C_{\#}\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|D^{2}f_{k}\|_{\infty}=C_{\#}A
,

(6.2)

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\|\leq\vartheta^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\|\nabla h\|+\bar{D}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\leq(\vartheta^{-1}a+\bar{D}){\mathcal{L}}_{k}h

which implies
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}{\mathcal{C}}_{a}\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{\nu a}
,
\nu\in(\vartheta^{-1},1)
, for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, provided
a\geq\bar{D}(\nu-\vartheta^{-1})^{-1}
.

From this point forward, we fix
\nu\in(\vartheta^{-1},1)
and
a>1
such that
a>\bar{D}(\nu-\vartheta)^{-1}
.

Now for each
h\in C^{1}
, and using (
6.1
) since both

\|h\|
and
h+\|h\|
are in
{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
,

(6.3)

\begin{split}|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}h|&\leq|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(h+\|h\|)|+\|h\||{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}1|\\
&\leq e^{a}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}(h+2\|h\|)=3e^{a}\|h\|,\end{split}

which, iterating (
6.2
), proves (C-
4
).

One can easily compute the Hilber metric
d_{H}
and prove that for all
g\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\nu a}
,
\nu\in(0,1)
,

(6.4)

d_{H}(h,1)\leq 2a+\ln\frac{1+\nu}{1-\nu}=:\Delta/2.

So (C-
2
) holds true.

6.2.
Conditions (O-
1
), (O-
2
) (Applicability of Theorem
2.6
)

Assume that we have a sequence of observables
g_{k}
satisfying
\sup_{k}(\|g_{k}\|_{\infty}+\|\nabla g_{k}\|_{\infty})=K<\infty
. This implies in particular that
\sup_{k}\|g_{k}\|\leq(1+a^{-1})K
, hence (O-
1
) holds with
j_{0}=\infty
.

Let
\rho\in C^{1}
,
\rho\geq 0
with
\int\rho\,d\mu_{0}=1
.
Define
\hat{g}_{k}
as in (
2.8
). For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
define
\hat{S}_{n}
as in
(
2.10
) and the variance
\sigma_{n}^{2}
as in (
2.11
).
Remark that
\sigma_{n}\leq C_{\#}\sqrt{n}
by Lemma
2.4
.

With
n
fixed and
k\leq n
, define the weighted operators for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
as in
(
2.14
),

{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}h:={\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}h={\mathcal{L}}_{k}(e^{i\sigma_{n}^{-1}\lambda g_{k}}h)\,.

Taking the derivative as in (
6.2
) we have

\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}h\|\leq\vartheta^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{k,0}\|\nabla h\|+(\bar{D}+|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|g_{k}\|_{\infty}){\mathcal{L}}_{k,0}|h|.

Which can be iterated, yielding

\begin{split}\|\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j,\lambda}h\|&\leq\vartheta^{-k+j+1}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\|\nabla h\|+\sum_{l=0}^{k-j}\vartheta^{-l}(\bar{D}+|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|g_{k-l}\|_{\infty}){\mathcal{L}}_{k-l}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{j}|h|\\
&\leq\|h\|\frac{3e^{a}(\bar{D}+|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}\sup_{j}\|g_{j}\|_{\infty})}{1-\vartheta^{-1}}.\end{split}

where we have used (
6.3
) in the last step. Applying (
6.3
) again
proves (O-
2
).

Accordingly, Theorem
2.6
applies.
Note that Theorem
2.6
provides nontrivial information only if
\sigma_{n}\geq C_{\#}n^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>\frac{1}{3}
.

6.3.
Complex Cones for Expanding Maps

To obtain the sharper results of Theorem
2.7
, we have to verify condition (O-
3
). In order to do that, we use the complex cone theory introduced by Rugh and further developed by Dubois
[
35
,
16
,
17
]
,
as summarized in Section
5
.

First we need to verify that we are in the setting of Section
5
.
As noted earlier, with
\mathbbm{e}=1
, the norm associated the cone is
\|h\|=\sup_{x}\{|h(x)|+a^{-1}\|\nabla h(x)\|\}
which is equivalent to the
C^{1}
norm, so
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}=C^{1}(M,{\mathbb{R}})
.

For each
x\in M
and
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
\|v\|\leq 1
, we define

\ell_{x,v}(h)=ah(x)-\langle v,\nabla h(x)\rangle.

Clearly
\ell_{x,v}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
.
In addition, it is easy to check that

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{C}}_{a}=\{h\in{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathbb{R}}\;:\;\ell(h)\geq 0\;\forall\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}\}\\
&{\mathcal{S}}=\{\ell_{x,v}\;:\;x\in M,\|v\|\leq 1\}\end{split}

This shows that (
5.1
) and (
5.7
) are satisfied.
The complex cone
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
is defined precisely as in Definition
5.3
.

Next, set
\mathbbm{m}(h)=\int h
, then, recalling (
6.1
), we have, for all
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
,

(6.5)

\mathbbm{m}(h)\geq e^{-a}\|h\|_{\infty}\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{-a}\|h\|:=\kappa\|h\|\,,

which verifies (
5.6
), and by extension (
5.11
).

To conclude checking the hypotheses of Theorem
5.15
we must to prove that the diameter of the image of the complex cone is finite. Since (
6.4
) states that the diameter of the real cone is finite, we can apply Theorem
5.17
, provided we check (
5.23
).
To check (
5.23
) let
\ell_{x,v}\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
,

(6.6)

\begin{split}\left|\ell_{x,v}\left(({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})h\right)\right|&\leq\left|({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})\langle(Df)^{-1}v,\nabla h\rangle\right|+\left|({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k}){\mathcal{D}}_{v}h\right|\\
&+\left|{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}(i\lambda\sigma_{n}^{-1}\langle(Df)^{-1}v,\nabla g_{k}\rangle h)\right|+a\left|({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})h\right|,\end{split}

where
{\mathcal{D}}_{v}=-\operatorname{sign}(\det Df_{k})\frac{\langle Df_{k}^{-1}v,\nabla\det Df_{k}\rangle}{(\det Df_{k})^{2}}
.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}1\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
, (
6.1
) implies
the following bound on the first term above,

\begin{split}\vartheta^{-1}\left|({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})\|\nabla h\|\right|&\leq\vartheta^{-1}|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}|g_{k}|_{\infty}|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}1|_{\infty}\|\nabla h\|_{\infty}\leq\vartheta^{-1}ae^{a}|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}|g_{k}|_{\infty}|h|_{\infty}\\
&\leq\vartheta^{-1}ae^{2a}|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}|g_{k}|_{\infty}\int h\leq\vartheta^{-1}ae^{2a}|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}|g_{k}|_{\infty}\int{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\\
&\leq\vartheta^{-1}ae^{3a}|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}|g_{k}|_{\infty}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h(x)\,,\end{split}

where we have used
h,{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
. Similarly, we bound the second, third and fourth terms
of (
6.6
) using the positivity of
h
,

|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}(\bar{D}+a)|g_{k}|_{\infty}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h(x)+\vartheta^{-1}|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|\nabla g_{k}\|_{\infty}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h(x)\,.

Putting these estimates together in (
6.6
) implies,

\left|\ell_{x,v}\left(({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})h\right)\right|\leq|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}\|g_{k}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\left[\vartheta^{-1}ae^{3a}+\bar{D}+a\right]{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h(x).

On the other hand,
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\nu a}
, hence

\ell_{x,v}({\mathcal{L}}_{k}h)=a{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h-\langle v,\nabla{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\rangle\geq a(1-\nu){\mathcal{L}}_{k}h(x).

Accordingly,

\left|\ell_{x,v}\left(({\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda}-{\mathcal{L}}_{k})h\right)\right|\leq|\lambda|\sigma_{n}^{-1}a^{-1}(1-\nu)^{-1}\|g_{k}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\left[\vartheta^{-1}ae^{3a}+\bar{D}+a\right]\ell_{x,v}({\mathcal{L}}_{k}h).

which satisfies (
5.23
) provided
\lambda
is small enough. Hence Theorem
5.17
proves the finite diameter of the complex cone, and Theorem
5.15
applies.

6.4.
Verification of Condition (O-
3
)

We can finally check condition (O-
3
).
By the above discussion it follows that, for
\lambda_{0}
small enough, and
|\lambda|\leq\lambda_{0}\sigma_{n}
, we have that Theorem
5.15
applies to each
{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}
with complex diameter
\Delta_{{\mathbb{C}}}<\infty
uniform in
\lambda
,
k
and
n
.
Moreover, since
{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathbb{R}},k}={\mathcal{C}}_{a}
and
\mathbbm{m}_{k}=\mathbbm{m}=\mu
, the Riemannian volume,
for each
k
, we are in the setting of Section
5.3
with
\bar{\kappa}=\kappa=\frac{1}{2}e^{-a}
,
K
the constant from (O-
2
) and
{\mathbb{L}}_{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{k,\lambda,n}
.
We can then apply Lemma
5.20
to obtain (O-
3
) with

\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{k,j}
,
h_{k,j,\lambda}=\boldsymbol{h}_{k,j}

and
\ell_{k,j,\lambda}=\boldsymbol{\ell}_{k,j}
, as defined in Section
5.3
.

By definition
\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(1)=1
and
\mathbbm{m}(h_{k,j,\lambda})=1
.
Note that this normalization is compatible with that required in (O-
3
)
since
\mathbbm{m}(h_{k,j,\lambda})=\int h_{k,j,\lambda}
.
Moreover,
|\alpha_{k,j,\lambda}|\leq 1
and
\|h_{k,j,\lambda}\|\leq\sqrt{2}/\kappa
,

\|\ell_{k,j,\lambda}\|^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{2}
by Lemma
5.19
.
Note that all these bounds are independent of
n
.

Then since condition (b) of Section
5.3
holds with
K=1
,
Lemma
5.20

implies that the property (
2.15
) of (O-
3
) holds with
K=\frac{2(1+\sqrt{2})}{\kappa\theta^{2}}\Delta_{{\mathbb{C}}}
,

\theta=\tanh(\Delta_{{\mathbb{C}}}/4)

and
c=-\log\theta
.

Since we have already verified the hypotheses of Theorem
5.17
, conditions (d) and (e) of Section
5.4
are satisfied.
Thus the final claim that there exists
K>0
such that
|\ell_{k,j,\lambda}(h_{j,l,\lambda})|\geq K^{-1}
for all
0\leq l\leq j\leq k\leq n
, follows immediately from Lemma
5.21
.

6.5.
Central Limit Theorem for Sequential Expanding Maps

We have verified hypotheses (C-
1
)-(C-
4
) and
(O-
1
)-(O-
3
). Thus Theorem
2.7

and Corollary
2.9
apply to the setting of sequential expanding maps. Hence, Theorem
2.1
follows.

As mentioned in Section
2.1
, Theorem
2.1
is conditional on the growth of
\sigma_{n}
.
To find conditions implying that the variance grows sufficiently fast, in the abstract sequential case setting, is nonobvious.
Here, we provide a partial answer for the case at hand.

To simplify matters, we assume that the maps and observables appearing in the sequence all belong to a finite set
21
21

21

The finiteness allows us to obtain a condition that can be checked on a finite number of finite time trajectories; otherwise some type of compactness seems to be needed.

\begin{split}&{\mathcal{F}}:=\{\bar{f}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\;\quad\lambda=\inf_{j}\inf_{x}\|(D_{x}\bar{f}_{j})^{-1}\|^{-1}>1\\
&{\mathcal{G}}=\{\bar{g}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}\subset{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(M,{\mathbb{R}}).\end{split}

For any given sequences
\omega^{f},\omega^{g}\in\{1,\dots,N\}^{{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}}
, we set
f_{k}=\bar{f}_{\omega^{f}_{k}}
,
g_{k}=\bar{g}_{\omega^{g}_{k}}
.

Such maps satisfy specification: for each
\varepsilon,L>0
, there exists
D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)
such that for each set of intervals
J_{k}=\{a_{k},\dots,a_{k}+L\}
, such that
a_{k+1}-a_{k}-L\geq D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)
, and points
x_{k}\in{\mathbb{T}}
, there exists
z\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that

|f_{a_{k}+i}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(z)-f_{a_{k}+i}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(x_{k})|\leq\varepsilon,

for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
i\in\{0,\dots,L-1\}
.

The main result in this section is the following criterion.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\ln\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(M)
. If there exists
a>0
,
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for each sequence
\{\omega_{i}\}_{i=0}^{L}
there exists a point
x_{\omega}
such that

\begin{split}&\sum_{i=1}^{L}\hat{g}_{\omega_{i}}(\bar{f}_{\omega_{i-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\bar{f}_{\omega_{0}}(x_{\omega}))\geq aL\\
&\varepsilon\leq\min\left\{\varepsilon_{0},\frac{a}{4\sup_{j}\|D\bar{g}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}}\right\}\;;\quad L\geq\max\left\{\frac{4}{a}D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)\max_{j}\|\hat{g}_{j}\|_{\infty},D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)\right\}.\end{split}

then there exists
B>0
such that
\sigma_{n}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}(S_{n}^{2})\geq Bn
.

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of the above Proposition. But first, it is appropriate to discuss its relevance and generality.

Remark 6.2
.

For simplicity, in Proposition
6.1
, we assume that
\ln\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
. This is done so that

\rho
belongs to a real cone
{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
, as defined in Section
6.1
.
The interested reader can generalize to the case
\rho\in C^{1}
,
\rho\geq 0
, since then there exists a
k_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho\in{\mathcal{C}}_{a}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.

Remark 6.3
.

Note that in the case of a single dynamical system and a single observable (that is
\bar{f}_{k}=f
, and
g_{k}=g
, a zero average observable), the usual condition for the linear growth of the variance is that there exists a periodic orbit
\{p,f(p),\dots,f^{q-1}(p)\}
,
f^{q}(p)=p
, such that
22
22

22

Possibly substituting
-g
for
g
.

\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}g(f^{k}(p))\geq b
, for some
b>0
.
23
23

23

Which implies that
g
is not a coboundary.
This implies the condition in Proposition
6.1
. To see it choose
\mu_{k}
to be the invariant measure of
f
, so
\hat{g}=g
. Then, setting
L=Kq
,
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{L}g(f^{i-1}(p))=K\sum_{i=1}^{q}g(f^{i-1}(p))\geq Kb=L\frac{b}{q}=:aL.

Accordingly, the conditions of Proposition
6.1
are satisfied with the choice
x_{\omega}=p
. This shows that Proposition
6.1
is a natural generalization to the sequential case of the usual condition. It remains to see if the condition is explicitly checkable; this is verified in Corollary
6.4
.

Note that, by equation (
6.9
),
\|\hat{g}_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|g_{k}\|_{\infty}
.
Moreover, the
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
have a uniform spectral gap. Hence, there exists a computable
A>0
,
\sigma\in(0,1)
such that, for all
S\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{M}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho-\gamma_{S,k}\right|\leq A\sigma^{S}\\
&\gamma_{S,k}:=\frac{1}{\int_{M}1}\int_{M}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{k-S}1.\end{split}

Let
\Gamma=\sup_{j}\max\{\|Dg_{j}\|_{\infty},2\|g_{j}\|_{\infty}\}
and, for some
a\in(0,1)
to be chosen later small enough, we choose
\varepsilon=\frac{a}{4\Gamma}
. Then, by Lemma
6.6
, there exists
c_{\star}>0
such that
D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)=c_{\star}\ln a^{-1}
. Accordingly, we can choose
L=\frac{4\Gamma c_{\star}\ln a^{-1}}{a}
. Finally, we choose
S
so that
A\sigma^{S}\leq a
. With such choices, we imediatly have:

Corollary 6.4
.

If we there exists
a>0
such that, for each sequence
\{\omega_{i}\}_{i=0}^{L}
, there exists a point
x_{\omega}
such that

(6.7)

\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{g}_{\omega_{i}}(\bar{f}_{\omega_{i-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\bar{f}_{\omega_{0}}(x_{\omega}))\geq 2aL,

where
\tilde{g}_{k}=g_{k}-\gamma_{S,k}
, then the hypothesis of Proposition
6.1
are verified. Thus,
\sigma_{n}^{2}\geq Bn
.

Note that condition (
6.7
) is checkable by trial and error. This is similar to the problem of finding a periodic orbit with non-zero average in the nonsequential case.

To prove Proposition
6.1
, we will use a martingale decomposition,
similar to
[
14
]
, but we must first introduce some notation.
Let

(6.8)

h_{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho

and
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(g)=h_{k+1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(h_{k}g)
. Note that
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(g)=h_{k+1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{i}(h_{i}g)
, moreover
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}1=1
. Also, recalling (
2.8
),

(6.9)

\hat{g}_{k}=g_{k}-\int_{M}g_{k}{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho

We would like to find
\phi_{k},Y_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
such that

(6.10)

\begin{split}&\hat{g}_{k}=\phi_{k+1}\circ f_{k}-\phi_{k}+Y_{k}\\
&\phi_{0}=0\\
&\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}Y_{k}=0.\end{split}

This decomposition would imply,

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\hat{g}_{k}=\phi_{k+1}-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\phi_{k}\,.

With this motivation, let us define
\phi_{0}=0
and for each
k\geq 1
,

(6.11)

\phi_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\hat{g}_{j},

and set
Y_{k}=\hat{g}_{k}-\phi_{k+1}\circ f_{k}+\phi_{k}
. Then indeed,

\begin{split}-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}Y_{k}=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\left[\phi_{k+1}\circ f_{k}-\phi_{k}-\hat{g}_{k}\right]&=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left[\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\hat{g}_{j}\right]-\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\cdots\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{j}\hat{g}_{j}-\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\hat{g}_{k}=0.\end{split}

Note that, by usual arguments, there exists
C_{\star}>0
such that

(6.12)

\begin{split}&\|\phi_{k}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\leq C_{\star}\,,\;\;\|Y_{k}\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{1}}\leq C_{\star}\\
&h_{k}\geq C_{\star}^{-1}.\end{split}

Let
S_{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\hat{g}_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
. We want to compute
24
24

24

As usual, by
{\mathbb{E}}(\varphi)
we mean
\int_{M}\varphi(x)\,\rho\,dx
.

{\mathbb{E}}(S_{n}^{2})={\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}Y_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right]^{2}\right)

Note that, recalling (
6.8
) and also (
6.10
), for all
k<n
,

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}&\left(\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\cdot Y_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right)=\int\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k}\cdot Y_{k}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\rho\,dx\\
&=\int\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k}\cdot Y_{k}h_{k}\,dx\\
&=\int\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{k+1}\cdot h_{k+1}\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k}(Y_{k})\,dx=0\end{split}

Accordingly, recalling (
6.12
) and (
6.10
),

(6.13)

\begin{split}{\mathbb{E}}(S_{n}^{2})&={\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}Y_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right]^{2}\right)\\
&={\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}Y_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}\right]^{2}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left(h_{k}Y_{k}^{2}\right)+2\sum_{k>i}{\mathbb{E}}\left(h_{i}Y_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{i}Y_{i}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left(h_{k}Y_{k}^{2}\right)+2\sum_{k>i}{\mathbb{E}}\left(h_{k}Y_{k}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}\cdots\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{i}Y_{i}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left(h_{k}Y_{k}^{2}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(1).\end{split}

Next, we want to connect the growth of
S_{n}
along a single orbit with the growth of its
L^{2}
norm.

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that there exists
p\in{\mathbb{T}}
such that
|S_{n}(p)|\geq 2An^{\alpha}
,
\alpha\geq 2/3
. Then
{\mathbb{E}}(S_{n}^{2})\geq Bn^{-2+3\alpha}
, for some
B>0
.

Proof.

We have

\begin{split}|S_{n}(p)|&=\left|\phi_{n+1}\circ f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p)+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}Y_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p)\right|\\
&\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}|Y_{k}|\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p)+{\mathcal{O}}(1).\end{split}

Note that, for each
q\in{\mathbb{T}}
,
|Y_{k}(x)|\geq|Y_{k}(q)|-C_{\star}|x-q|
. If follows that, for all
x\in\{y\in{\mathbb{T}}\;:\;|y-q|\leq|Y_{k}(q)|/(2C_{\star})\}
, one has
|Y_{k}(x)|\geq\frac{1}{2}|Y_{k}(q)|
.
Thus

{\mathbb{E}}(h_{k}Y_{k}^{2})={\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0})\geq\frac{|Y_{k}|^{3}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p)}{8C_{\star}}.

Let
J_{n}=\{k\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}\;:\;|Y_{k}|\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p)\geq An^{-1+\alpha}\}
, then
using (
6.13
),

\begin{split}2An^{\alpha}&\leq|S_{n}(p)|\leq\frac{2n^{2-2\alpha}}{A^{2}}\sum_{k\in J_{n}}|Y_{k}|^{3}\circ f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p)+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\\
&\leq\frac{16C_{\star}n^{2-2\alpha}}{A^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbb{E}}(h_{k}Y_{k}^{2})+{\mathcal{O}}(1)\leq\frac{16C_{\star}n^{2-2\alpha}}{A^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}(S_{n}^{2})+{\mathcal{O}}(n^{2-2\alpha}).\end{split}

∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Let
D:=D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)
as in Lemma
6.6
. For any
\omega^{f},\omega^{g}\in\{1,\dots N\}^{{\mathbb{N}}\cap\{0\}}
, let
f_{k}=\bar{f}_{\omega^{f}_{k}}
and
g_{k}=\bar{g}_{\omega^{g}_{k}}
. By hypothesis there exists
p\in M
such that

\begin{split}S_{n}(p)&\geq\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{L+D}\rfloor}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\hat{g}_{a_{k}+i+1}(f_{a_{k}+i}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}(p))-\left[\frac{nD}{L+D}+1\right]\|\hat{g}\|_{\infty}\\
&\geq\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{L+D}\rfloor}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\hat{g}_{a_{k}+i+1}(f_{a_{k}+i}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(x_{k}))-\frac{\varepsilon Ln}{L+D}\|Dg\|_{{\mathcal{C}}^{0}}-\frac{n}{L+D}D\|\hat{g}\|_{\infty}\\
&\geq\frac{aLn}{L+D}-\frac{aLn}{4(L+D)}-\frac{anL}{4(L+D)}\geq\frac{a}{4}n.\end{split}

We can then apply Lemma
6.5
with
\alpha=1
, which proves the proposition.
∎

It remains to discuss the Lemma used to obtain Corollary
6.4
.

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that, for all
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, we can choose
D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)=\inf\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\;:\;\varepsilon\lambda^{n}\geq\operatorname{diam}(M)\}
.

Proof.

Set
\varepsilon_{0}
so that all the maps
\bar{f}_{j}
are locally invertible on balls of size
\varepsilon_{0}
.
For
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, consider the set

A_{k}=\{y\in M\;:\;|f_{a_{k}+i}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(y)-f_{a_{k}+i}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(x_{k})|\leq\varepsilon,\,0\leq i\leq L-1\}.

Then
f_{a_{k}+L-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(A_{k})
is a ball of radius
\varepsilon
. Accordingly,

f_{a_{k}+D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(A_{k})=M.

Thus
f_{a_{k}+D_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\varepsilon,L)}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a_{k}}(A_{k})\supset A_{k+1}
. Taking the intersection of the preimages of the
A_{k}
, we have a nested sequence of closed sets and we can choose as
z
any point in the intersection.
∎

7.
Application: Sequential Billiard Maps

In this section, we show that Theorems
2.6
and
2.7
apply to sequential billiards
as described in
[
8
]
. Our strategy will be to define real and complex cones on which
the relevant operators act as strict contractions, and so apply the theorems of Section
5
to
verify conditions (O-
1
)-(O-
3
).
We begin by recalling the setting of
[
8
]
.

Fixing constants
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\ell_{i}>0
,
i=1,\ldots K
, we consider billiard configurations in

\mathbb{T}^{2}=\mathbb{R}^{2}/\mathbb{Z}^{2}

formed by placing
K
scatterers
B_{i}
having
C^{3}
boundaries and with arclengths given by
\ell_{i}

and curvatures
{\mathcal{K}}>0
. We denote such a billiard table by
Q=\mathbb{T}^{2}\setminus\cup_{i}B_{i}
. The billiard map
T

is defined by the motion of a point particle undergoing elastic collisions at the boundaries
and traveling with unit speed in straight lines between collisions. We adopt the standard collision
coordinates,
x=(r,\varphi)
, where
r
is the arclength coordinate on
\partial Q
and
\varphi
is the angle
made by the post-collision velocity with the normal to the boundary.
Thus the phase space for
T
is given by
{\mathcal{M}}=\cup_{i=1}^{K}I_{i}\times[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}]
, where for each
i
,
I_{i}=[0,\ell_{i}]/\sim
is an interval
of length
\ell_{i}
with endpoints identified. Since we have fixed
K
and
\ell_{i}
,
{\mathcal{M}}
is the same
for each such table
Q
.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}_{\min}(Q)\leq{\mathcal{K}}_{\max}(Q)
denote the minimum and maximum curvatures of scatterers
in the table
Q
, and let
\tau_{\min}(Q)\leq\tau_{\max}(Q)
denote the minimum and maximum
distances between consecutive collisions.

These scatterer configurations are subject to the following three constraints:
Fix
\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*}>0
. Then for each configuration
Q
, we assume:

\tau_{*}\leq\tau_{\min}(Q)\leq\tau_{\max}(Q)\leq\tau_{*}^{-1}
,

{\mathcal{K}}_{*}\leq{\mathcal{K}}_{\min}(Q)\leq{\mathcal{K}}_{\max}(Q)\leq{\mathcal{K}}_{*}^{-1}
,
the
C^{3}
norm of
\partial Q
is at most
E_{*}
.
Let
{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
denote the set of billiard tables with
K
scatterers satisfying these conditions
and let

{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
denote the corresponding set of billiard maps.
As noted above, each
T\in{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
acts on the same phase space
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Fixing these constants ensures that maps in
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
enjoy the uniform
properties listed as
(H1)
-
(H5)
in
[
8
]
: a common set of invariant cones in
the tangent space, uniform distortion bounds, uniform growth lemma, etc.

We choose as our reference measure on
{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}:=\frac{1}{2|\partial Q|}\cos\varphi\,drd\varphi
,
which is preserved by each
T\in{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
. The associated transfer operator

{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
acting on measurable functions is defined by,

{\mathcal{L}}_{T}h=h\circ T^{-1}\,.

In order to generate sufficient hyperbolicity to strictly contract the cones
{\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)
defined
in Section
7.1
below,
we require a notion of distance in
{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
. For a table
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
,
the boundary
\partial B_{i}
of each scatterer can be parametrized according to arclength by a function

u_{i,\theta}
, where
\theta\in[0,\ell_{i})
represents the tagged point in
\partial B_{i}

where the parametrization begins. Given two tables
Q,\tilde{Q}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
with
respective parametrizations
u_{i,\theta}
,
\tilde{u}_{i,\theta}
, let
\Pi_{K}
denote the set of permutations

\pi
on
\{1,\ldots,K\}
satisfying
\tilde{\ell}_{\pi(i)}=\ell_{i}
. Define the distance,

\mathbbm{d}(Q,\tilde{Q})=\min_{\pi\in\Pi_{K}}\min_{\theta\in[0,\ell_{i})}\sum_{i=1}^{K}|u_{i,\theta}-\tilde{u}_{\pi(i),\theta}|_{C^{2}(I_{i},\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

For
Q_{0}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
and
\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\{\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*}\}
, define

{\mathcal{Q}}(Q_{0},E_{*};\varepsilon)=\{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*}/2,{\mathcal{K}}_{*}/2,E_{*}):\mathbbm{d}(Q,Q_{0})<\varepsilon\}.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}(Q_{0},E_{*};\varepsilon)
denote the corresponding set of billiard maps.

Definition 7.1
.

Fix
Q_{k}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
,
\varepsilon>0
from
[
8
, Lemma 6.6(b)]
and
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}\in\mathbb{N}

from
[
8
, Theorem 2.3]
.
We define each of our maps
f_{k}
(in the notation of Section
2
) by

f_{k}=T_{N_{\mathcal{F}}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{1}
, where
T_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}(Q_{k},E_{*};\varepsilon)
.

With this definition of
f_{k}
, the sequence
f_{k,0}=f_{k-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{0}
is necessarily an

N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
-admissible sequence in the terminology of
[
8
, Definition 2.4]
.

Note that each

f_{k}
is comprised of a collection of
N_{\mathcal{F}}
billiard maps all of which are close with respect to the
distance
\mathbbm{d}
, but the maps comprising
f_{k+1}
are not necessarily close to the maps
comprising
f_{k}
.
25
25

25

The notion of
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
-admissible sequence can also be simplified by choosing
f_{k}=T_{k}^{N_{{\mathcal{F}}}}
,
where
T_{k}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
, i.e. simply requiring
N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
iterates of the same map before changing
to a different (but not necessarily close) map.

In the notation of Section
2
then, we have
M_{k}={\mathcal{M}}
,
\mu_{k}=\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}
, while

f_{k}=T_{N_{\mathcal{F}}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{1}
, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{T_{N_{\mathcal{F}}}}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{T_{1}}
, where
T_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}(Q_{k},E_{*};\varepsilon)

as in Definition
7.1
.

7.1.
Definition and Contraction of the Real Cone
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}={\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)

Next, we must define precisely the cone of functions
constructed in
[
8
]
which is strictly contracted by
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
.

According to
[
8
, Section 3.1]
, there exist stable and unstable cones
C^{s}
and
C^{u}

in the tangent space of
{\mathcal{M}}

(not to be confused with the projective cones of functions defined below), that are strictly contracted by

DT^{-1}
and
DT
, respectively, for all
T\in{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
.
For fixed
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, define the usual homogeneity strips,

\mathbb{H}_{\pm k}=\{(r,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{M}}:(k+1)^{-2}\leq|\pm\tfrac{\pi}{2}-\varphi|\leq k^{-2}\},\;\;\mbox{for all $k\geq k_{0}$.}

For convenience, label
\mathbb{H}_{0}={\mathcal{M}}\setminus\cup_{|k|\geq k_{0}}\mathbb{H}_{k}
.
We say a
C^{1}
curve
W\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is stable or cone-stable if its tangent vector at each point lies in
C^{u}
. We call it
homogeneous if it lies in a single homogeneity strip.

Let
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
denote the set of homogeneous cone-stable curves whose curvature is bounded by
B_{0}>0
. We choose

B_{0}
so that
T^{-1}{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\subset{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
for all
T\in{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
.

Since we will subdivide curves when they cross
\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{k}
, we will refer to the extended
singularity sets for
T
(respectively
T^{-1}
) as
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{{\mathbb{H}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cup T^{-1}({\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cup_{|k|\geq k_{0}}\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{k})
(respectively
{\mathcal{S}}_{-1}^{{\mathbb{H}}}={\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cup T({\mathcal{S}}_{0}\cup_{|k|\geq k_{0}}\partial{\mathbb{H}}_{k})
),
where
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}=\{(r,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{M}}:\varphi=\pm\pi/2\}
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
a\geq 1
and
W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
, we define the following cone of test functions,
following
[
8
, Section 4.1]
. Let
d(\cdot,\cdot)
define distance on
W
induced by arclength.

{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\alpha}(W):=\left\{\psi\in C^{0}(W):\psi>0,\frac{\psi(x)}{\psi(y)}\leq e^{ad(x,y)^{\alpha}}\right\}

To define the cone for
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
, we shall need a notion of distance between stable curves. To this end, we view
each
W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
as the graph of a
C^{2}
function of the
r
coordinate,

(7.1)

W=\{G_{W}(r)=(r,\varphi_{W}(r)):r\in I_{W}\}.

For
W^{1},W^{2}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
, if
W^{1}
and
W^{2}
lie in the same homogeneity strip with
|I_{W^{1}}\cap I_{W^{2}}|>0
, define the distance between them by

d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})=|\varphi_{W^{1}}-\varphi_{W^{2}}|_{C^{1}(I_{W^{1}}\cap I_{W^{2}})}+|I_{W^{1}}\bigtriangleup I_{W^{2}}|.

Otherwise, define
d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})=\infty
. Although
d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}
is not a metric, it is sufficient for our needs.

When
d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})<\infty
, we will also need to measure a distance between test functions. For

\beta<\alpha
as defined below and
\psi_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W^{i})
, define,

d_{*}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=\Big|\,\psi_{1}\circ G_{W^{1}}\|G^{\prime}_{W^{1}}\|-\psi_{2}\circ G_{W^{2}}\|G^{\prime}_{W^{2}}\|\,\Big|_{C^{\beta}(I_{W^{1}}\cap I_{W^{2}})}\,,

where
\|G^{\prime}_{W}\|=\sqrt{1+(d\varphi_{W}/dr)^{2}}
.

For a given length scale
\delta
, define

{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{-}(\delta)=\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}:|W|\leq 2\delta\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta)=\{W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}:|W|\in[\delta,2\delta]\}\,.

Next, let
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
denote the set of functions on
{\mathcal{M}}
whose restriction to each
W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}

is integrable with respect to the arclength measure
dm_{W}
. Define,

{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}^{\sim}=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{-}(\delta)}}{{\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W)}}}\frac{|\int_{W}h\psi\,dm_{W}|}{\int_{W}\psi\,dm_{W}},\quad\mbox{for }h\in{\mathcal{A}}_{*}.

Set
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}=\{h\in{\mathcal{A}}_{*}:{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}^{\sim}<\infty\}
, and note that
{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}\cdot{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}^{\sim}

defines a seminorm on
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
. Thus identifying two functions
g
and
h
in
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
if

{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}g-h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}^{\sim}=0
, we define
{\mathcal{A}}
to be the resulting normed vector space of equivalence classes.
Note that if
g\sim h
, then
g=h
almost everywhere with respect to both the Lebesgue measure and

\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}
.

Next, for
h\in{\mathcal{A}}
, define the following two quantities,

(7.2)

{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}=\sup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta)}}{{\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W)}}}\frac{\left|\int_{W}h\psi\,dm_{W}\right|}{\int_{W}\psi\,dm_{W}},\hskip 36.98866pt{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{-}=\inf_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta)}}{{\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W)}}}\frac{\int_{W}h\psi\,dm_{W}}{\int_{W}\psi\,dm_{W}}.

Denote the average value of

\psi
on
W
by
\fint_{W}\psi\,dm_{W}=\frac{1}{|W|}\int_{W}\psi\,dm_{W}
.
Since all of our integrals on
W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}

will be taken with respect to the arc-length
dm_{W}
, to keep our notation concise, we will drop the measure from our
integral notation in the following.

For exponents,
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,q\in(0,1)
and constants
a,c,A,L>1
,
\delta>0
, we define the cone

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)=\Bigg\{

\displaystyle h\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{0\}:

(7.3)

\displaystyle{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}\leq L{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{-};

(7.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{W\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{-}(\delta)}\sup_{\psi\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W)}|W|^{-q}\frac{|\int_{W}h\psi|}{\fint_{W}\psi}\leq A\delta^{1-q}{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{-};

\displaystyle\forall\,W^{1},W^{2}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{-}(\delta):d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})\leq\delta,\forall\psi_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\alpha}(W^{i}):d_{*}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=0,

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\int_{W^{1}}h\psi_{1}}{\fint_{W^{1}}\psi_{1}}-\frac{\int_{W^{2}}h\psi_{2}}{\fint_{W^{2}}\psi_{2}}\right|\leq d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})^{\gamma}\,\delta^{1-\gamma}cA{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{-}\Bigg\}.

Let
\delta_{0}
be small enough so that
[
8
, eq. (3.8)]
holds true. Next,
we assume the parameters of the cone satisfy the constraints detailed in
[
8
, Section 5.3]
. In particular

(7.6)

\begin{split}&q\in(0,1/2),\quad 0<\beta<\alpha\leq 1/3,\quad\gamma\leq\min\{\alpha-\beta,q\}\\
&3\delta<\delta_{0},\quad e^{a2(\delta_{0})^{\beta}}\leq 2,\quad 4A\bar{C}_{0}\delta\leq\delta_{0}/4,\quad c>16C_{s}^{q}\end{split}

where
\bar{C}_{0}>1
is defined in
[
8
, Lemm 3.3]
and
C_{s}:=\sqrt{1+({\mathcal{K}}_{*}^{-1}+\tau_{*}^{-1})^{2}}
is the maximum slope of cone-stable curves in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
.
The fundamental property of the above cone is the following.

Theorem 7.2
.

[
8
, Theorem 6.12]

Fix constants
c,L
and
A
satisfying the conditions of
[
8
, Section 5.3]
. Then
there exists
\delta_{*}>0
such that for all
\delta\in(0,\delta_{*})

there exists

N_{{\mathcal{F}}}=N_{{\mathcal{F}}}(\delta)
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{T_{N_{\mathcal{F}}}}\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{T_{1}}({\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta))\subset{\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)
with uniformly finite diameter in
{\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)
for all choices of
Q_{k}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
with
T_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}(Q_{k},E_{*};\varepsilon)
,
j=1,\ldots,N_{{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Since all the parameters are fixed from here forward, to simplify notation and to match notation with
Section
5
, we will refer to
{\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)
simply as
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}
in what follows.

7.2.
Conditions (C-
1
)-(C-
4
)

Having defined the cone we can now check the conditions on the dynamics.

Proposition 7.3
.

Fix
\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*}>0
, and let
{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{*},{\mathcal{K}}_{*},E_{*})
be as defined above. Then
conditions (C-
1
)-(C-
4
) of Section
2
hold
with
M_{k}={\mathcal{M}}
,
\mu_{k}=\mu_{\mbox{\tiny SRB}}
,
C_{k}={\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)

and
f_{k}=T_{N_{\mathcal{F}}}\circ\cdots\circ T_{1}
,
T_{j}\in{\mathcal{F}}(Q_{k},E_{*};\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

Remark that since
C_{k}={\mathcal{C}}_{c,A,L}(\delta)
is the same for all
k
, then
\|\cdot\|_{k}=\|\cdot\|
is independent of
k
.

Condition (C-
1
) follows from
[
8
, Remark 7.2]
, while Condition (C-
2
) follows from
[
8
, Theorems 6.12 and 6.13]
.

Condition (C-
3
) instead follows from the beginning of the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 8.15]
where
\mathbbm{e}
is chosen to be the constant function
1
.

To prove condition (C-
4
) note that by choice of our reference measure, we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}1=1
. Hence,

-\|h\|\mathbbm{e}=-\|h\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\mathbbm{e}\preceq{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\preceq\|h\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}\mathbbm{e}\preceq\|h\|\mathbbm{e}

which implies
\|{\mathcal{L}}_{k}h\|\leq\|h\|
, which proves (C-
4
) with
C_{*}=1
.
∎

Before introducing the observables for which we will prove the CLT and verifying conditions
(O-
1
)-(O-
3
), we need to present a description of the cone adapted to the theory developed in Section
5
.

7.3.
Alternative Description of the Cone
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}

So that our notation for the cone
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}
coincides with the formulation in Section
5
, we
present the following equivalent description of
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}
in terms of the positivity of a set of linear functionals.
With the parameters
a,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,q,c,A,L,\delta
fixed as above,
define a corresponding set of linear functionals
{\mathcal{S}}
on
{\mathcal{A}}
as follows:

(7.7)

\ell_{W^{1},W^{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},L}(h)=L\frac{\int_{W^{1}}h\psi_{1}}{\int_{W^{1}}\psi_{1}}-\frac{\int_{W^{2}}h\psi_{2}}{\int_{W^{2}}\psi_{2}},\quad W^{i}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta),\psi_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W^{i}).

(7.8)

\displaystyle\ell_{W^{1},W^{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},q,\pm}(h)

\displaystyle=A\delta^{1-q}\frac{\int_{W^{0}}h\psi_{0}}{\int_{W^{0}}\psi_{0}}\pm|{W^{1}}|^{1-q}\frac{\int_{W^{1}}h\psi_{1}}{\int_{W^{1}}\psi_{1}},

\displaystyle\quad W^{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta),W^{1}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{-}(\delta),\psi_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W^{i}).

(7.9)

\displaystyle\ell_{W^{0},W^{1},W^{2},\psi_{0},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\gamma,\pm}(h)

\displaystyle=d_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})^{\gamma}\delta^{1-\gamma}cA\delta^{1-q}\frac{\int_{W^{0}}h\psi_{0}}{\int_{W^{0}}\psi_{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\pm\left[\frac{|W^{1}|\int_{W^{1}}h\psi_{2}}{\int_{W^{1}}\psi_{1}}-\frac{|W^{2}|\int_{W^{2}}h\psi_{3}}{\int_{W^{1}}\psi_{2}}\right],

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188ptW^{0}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta),\psi_{0}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W^{0}),W^{1},W^{2}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{-}(\delta),\psi_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\alpha}(W^{i}),

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188ptd_{{\mathcal{W}}^{s}}(W^{1},W^{2})\leq\delta,d_{*}(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=0.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
denote the collection of all such linear functionals as
W^{i}
and
\psi_{i}
range over the stated
sets of stable curves and test functions.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be as defined above. Then,

C_{\mathbb{R}}=\{h\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{0\}\;:\;\ell(h)\geq 0,\forall\ell\in{\mathcal{S}}\}.

Proof.

The equivalences between the three classes of linear functionals defined in (
7.7
)-(
7.9
)
and the three cone conditions (
7.3
)-(
7.5
) is immediate.

Consider, for example, (
7.3
). If
{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}\leq L{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}h{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{-}
, then by definition of
{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}\cdot{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{+}
and
{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}\cdot{|\>\!\!|\>\!\!|}_{-}
it follows that

\ell_{W^{1},W^{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},L}(h)\geq 0
for all
W^{1},W^{2}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\delta)
and
\psi_{i}\in{\mathcal{D}}_{a,\beta}(W^{i})
.
On the other hand if
\ell_{W^{1},W^{2},\psi_{1},\psi_{2},L}(h)\geq 0
for all such
W^{i}
and
\psi_{i}
, then
it follows that
